Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and D (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F and D.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node F is connected to: G, K, X, P.
    *   Node D is connected to: J, P.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from F to D must necessarily pass through one of D's direct neighbors, which are J and P. This means that all paths from F to D must use either the edge (J, D) or the edge (P, D) as their final step to reach D.

3.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to D:**
    *   If we remove the edge (J, D), D is still connected to P. There are paths from F to P (e.g., F-P), so F would still be connected to D via P (F-P-D).
    *   If we remove the edge (P, D), D is still connected to J. There are paths from F to J (e.g., F-G-J), so F would still be connected to D via J (F-G-J-D).

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since removing only one of the edges ((J, D) or (P, D)) does not disconnect F from D, we must remove at least two edges.
    If we remove both (J, D) and (P, D), node D becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between F and D.

5.  **Formulate the cut set:**
    The set of edges [(J, D), (P, D)] successfully disconnects F from D. This set contains 2 edges. Since we've established that 1 edge is not sufficient, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(D, J), (D, P)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['D', 'P'], ['D', 'J']]}

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are P and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect P from N. I cannot remove P or N.

First, I identify all direct and indirect paths between P and N.
P's immediate neighbors are D, J, O. Node D is a dead end for reaching N.
N's immediate neighbors are C, B, O.

All paths from P to N must pass through either J or O (from P's side).
All paths from N to P must pass through either C, B, or O (from N's side).

Let's list the main paths from P to N:

1.  **Paths through O:**
    *   P - O - N (Direct path)
    *   P - O - C - N
    *   P - O - J - I - C - N
    *   P - O - J - I - H - B - N
    *   P - O - J - M - L - E - B - N

2.  **Paths through J (that don't necessarily use O):**
    *   P - J - O - N (This path also uses O, so it's covered by the "Paths through O" group if O is removed)
    *   P - J - O - C - N (Also uses O)
    *   P - J - I - C - N
    *   P - J - I - H - B - N
    *   P - J - M - L - E - B - N
    *   P - J - K - X - G - H - B - N
    *   P - J - K - X - G - F - E - B - N

**Attempting a single-node cut:**
*   If I remove **J**: Paths P-O-N and P-O-C-N still exist.
*   If I remove **O**: Paths P-J-I-C-N, P-J-I-H-B-N, P-J-M-L-E-B-N, P-J-K-X-G-H-B-N, P-J-K-X-G-F-E-B-N still exist.
*   If I remove **C**: Paths P-O-N, P-J-O-N, P-J-I-H-B-N, P-J-M-L-E-B-N, P-J-K-X-G-H-B-N, P-J-K-X-G-F-E-B-N still exist.
*   If I remove **B**: Paths P-O-N, P-O-C-N, P-J-O-N, P-J-I-C-N still exist.
*   Removing any other single node (I, H, G, F, E, K, X, M, L, D, Y) would also leave multiple paths intact.

Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

**Attempting a two-node cut:**
Let's consider the nodes that are direct neighbors of P (J, O) and N (C, B, O).
Notice that node O is a common neighbor for both P and N. Node J is a neighbor of P.

Consider removing nodes **O** and **J**:
1.  **Remove O:**
    *   All paths starting with P-O are broken (P-O-N, P-O-C-N, P-O-J-...).
    *   Any path from P-J that uses O (like P-J-O-N) is also broken.
    *   After removing O, P can only reach the rest of the graph via J.
    *   N can only be reached via C or B.
    *   The remaining paths from P to N must now all go through J. These are:
        *   P - J - I - C - N
        *   P - J - I - H - B - N
        *   P - J - M - L - E - B - N
        *   P - J - K - X - G - H - B - N
        *   P - J - K - X - G - F - E - B - N

2.  **Remove J (with O already removed):**
    *   Since all remaining paths from P to N must start with P-J, removing J will break all of them.
    *   Therefore, P is completely disconnected from N.

The cut set [O, J] successfully disconnects P and N, and it consists of 2 nodes. Since a single-node cut is not possible, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [O, J]

Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are F and J.
2.  **Analyze connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node J is directly connected to two other nodes: P and M. The edges are (P, J) and (M, J).
    *   Node F is directly connected to G, K, X, and P.
3.  **Identify all paths between F and J:**
    To disconnect F from J, we need to break all possible paths between them. Let's list some representative paths:
    *   **Path 1:** F - P - J
    *   **Path 2:** F - G - H - I - P - J
    *   **Path 3:** F - P - B - E - M - J
    *   **Path 4:** F - G - H - C - B - E - M - J
    *   **Path 5:** F - G - H - I - C - B - E - M - J
    *   **Path 6:** F - P - O - C - B - E - M - J
    *   **Path 7:** F - P - O - N - B - E - M - J
    *   **Path 8:** F - P - I - C - B - E - M - J
4.  **Determine a minimal cut set:**
    *   Observe that all paths leading to J must use either the edge (P, J) or the edge (M, J), as these are the only two edges directly connected to J.
    *   If we remove both (P, J) and (M, J), node J will become completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist between F and J.
    *   This cut set, [(P, J), (M, J)], consists of 2 edges.
5.  **Check for a smaller cut set:**
    *   Can we disconnect F and J by removing only 1 edge? No, because J has two distinct connections. If we remove only one of (P, J) or (M, J), the other connection remains, and a path could still exist (e.g., if (P, J) is removed, paths ending in (M, J) like F-P-B-E-M-J could still exist).
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
6.  **Confirm the cut set:** Since we found a cut set of size 2, and we've established that 1 edge is not enough, the minimum number of edges is 2. The cut set [(P, J), (M, J)] effectively isolates J, thus disconnecting it from F.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(P, J), (M, J)]

Image name: PG_task2_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and N, without removing H or N themselves.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of H and N to understand the immediate connections:
Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G
Neighbors of N: Y, O, B, C

I will systematically identify all distinct paths from H to N and then determine the minimum set of nodes whose removal will break all these paths.

Let's categorize the paths based on the first node from H and the last node before N:

**Paths through C:**
1. H - C - N (This is a direct path through C)

**Paths through I (from H) to O or B (to N):**
2. H - I - J - M - O - N
3. H - I - J - D - P - O - N
4. H - I - J - P - O - N
5. H - I - P - O - N
(All these paths from I to N pass through O)

**Paths through X (from H) to O or B (to N):**
6. H - X - K - F - B - N
7. H - X - K - F - E - B - N
8. H - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
(These paths from X to N pass through B or O)

**Paths through G (from H) to O or B (to N):**
9. H - G - X - K - F - B - N
10. H - G - X - K - F - E - B - N
11. H - G - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
(These paths from G to N pass through B or O)

Now, let's analyze the "bottleneck" nodes. Any path from H to N must pass through one of H's neighbors (I, C, X, G) and eventually reach one of N's neighbors (Y, O, B, C) before reaching N. Node Y is only connected to N and O. If O is removed, Y cannot serve as a bridge from H to N. Therefore, the critical "gateway" nodes to N are C, O, and B.

To disconnect H and N, we must ensure that no path can reach N through C, O, or B.

Let's test for a cut set of size 1:
- If I remove C: Paths like H-I-J-M-O-N or H-X-K-F-B-N still exist.
- If I remove O: Paths like H-C-N or H-X-K-F-B-N still exist.
- If I remove B: Paths like H-C-N or H-I-J-M-O-N still exist.
- Removing any other single node (e.g., I, X, G, J, M, D, P, K, F, E, L, Y) would also leave at least one path intact (e.g., H-C-N).
Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

Let's test for a cut set of size 2:
I will consider pairs of the critical gateway nodes {C, O, B}:
1.  **Remove {C, O}**:
    *   Path H-C-N is broken by C.
    *   All paths through O (e.g., H-I-J-M-O-N, H-X-K-F-E-L-M-O-N) are broken by O.
    *   However, paths that go through B are still intact, for example: H - X - K - F - B - N.
    *   Thus, {C, O} is not a cut set.
2.  **Remove {C, B}**:
    *   Path H-C-N is broken by C.
    *   All paths through B (e.g., H-X-K-F-B-N) are broken by B.
    *   However, paths that go through O are still intact, for example: H - I - J - M - O - N.
    *   Thus, {C, B} is not a cut set.
3.  **Remove {O, B}**:
    *   All paths through O are broken by O.
    *   All paths through B are broken by B.
    *   However, the direct path H - C - N is still intact.
    *   Thus, {O, B} is not a cut set.

Since removing any two of the critical gateway nodes (C, O, B) leaves at least one path intact, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

Let's consider a cut set of size 3:
Consider the set {C, O, B}.
1.  **Removing C**: Breaks the direct path H-C-N.
2.  **Removing O**: Breaks all paths from H that lead to N via O (e.g., H-I-J-M-O-N, H-X-K-F-E-L-M-O-N, H-G-X-K-F-E-L-M-O-N).
3.  **Removing B**: Breaks all paths from H that lead to N via B (e.g., H-X-K-F-B-N, H-G-X-K-F-E-B-N).

With C, O, and B removed, no path can reach N from H. Each of these nodes is essential:
*   If C is not removed, H-C-N remains.
*   If O is not removed (assuming C and B are removed), H-I-J-M-O-N remains.
*   If B is not removed (assuming C and O are removed), H-X-K-F-B-N remains.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and the cut set is {C, O, B}.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, O, B]
--- Ground Truth ---

        *   Enqueue `(J, [K, M, J])`.

    *   Visited: `{K, X, F, M, G, E, J, L}`.

4.  **Level 3 (Length 3):**

    *   Dequeue `(E, [K, F, E])`. Neighbors of E: B, M, L, F. M, L, F are visited.
        *   Enqueue `(B, [K, F, E, B])`.

    *   Nodes directly connected to N are Y, O, C, B.
    *   Nodes directly connected to G are H, F, K, X.
3.  **Identify Critical Junctions for G:**
    *   Any path to G must pass through one of its direct neighbors: H, F, K, or X.
    *   Let's examine K and X:
        *   K is connected to F and G. This means any path from N to G that goes through K must first pass through F (e.g., N -> ... -> F -> K -> G).
        *   X is connected to F and G. This means any path from N to G that goes through X must first pass through F (e.g., N -> ... -> F -> X -> G).
    *   Therefore, all paths from N to G must ultimately pass through either H or F.
4.  **Formulate a Hypothesis for a Cut Set:** Based on the observation in step 3, if we remove both H and F, all paths to G would be severed.
5.  **Verify the Cut Set {H, F}:**
    *   If H is removed, all paths ending in H-G are broken.
    *   If F is removed, all paths ending in F-G are broken. Additionally, since K and X are only connected to F (besides G), removing F also breaks all paths that would go through K-G or X-G.
    *   Thus, removing both H and F ensures that no path can reach G from N.
6.  **Determine Minimum Size:** To confirm that {H, F} is a *minimum* cut set, I look for node-disjoint paths between N and G. If I can find *k* node-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut set size is at least *k*.
    *   Path 1: N - C - H - G (Intermediate nodes: C, H)
    *   Path 2: N - O - P - F - G (Intermediate nodes: O, P, F)
    *   These two paths are node-disjoint (excluding N and G themselves), as the set of intermediate nodes {C, H} and {O, P, F} have no common elements.
    *   Since there are at least two node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 2 ({H, F}) has been found, and the minimum possible size is at least 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect H from N. This is equivalent to finding the minimum edge cut between H and N. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify all edges connected to H and N to understand their immediate neighborhood.
Edges connected to H: (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)
Edges connected to N: (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N)

Next, I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between H and N.
1.  **Path 1: H - B - N**
    Edges in this path: `{(H, B), (B, N)}`

2.  **Path 2: H - I - C - N**
    Edges in this path: `{(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}`
    Let's check if Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.
    -   (H, B) is not in Path 2.
    -   (B, N) is not in Path 2.
    -   (H, I) is not in Path 1.
    -   (I, C) is not in Path 1.
    -   (C, N) is not in Path 1.
    These two paths are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Path 3: H - G - F - E - I - P - O - N**
    Edges in this path: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, N)}`
    Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
    -   Edges of Path 3: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, N)
    -   Edges of Path 1: (H, B), (B, N)
    -   Edges of Path 2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    All edges in Path 3 are distinct from the edges in Path 1 and Path 2. For example, (H,G) is not (H,B) or (H,I). (E,I) is not (H,I) or (I,C). (O,N) is not (B,N) or (C,N).
    Therefore, these three paths (Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3) are edge-disjoint.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal achieves this disconnection.
A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all its incident edges. Node H has three incident edges: (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H).
If I remove these three edges:
-   (G, H) is removed.
-   (B, H) is removed.
-   (I, H) is removed.
After removing these edges, node H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between H and N. This set of edges successfully disconnects H from N.

This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by the edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]

        Edges: (M, E), (E, B)
    *   **Path 2 (P2): M - J - P - O - N - B**

        Edges: (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, B)

    *   Edges in P1: (M,E), (E,B)
    *   Edges in P2: (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B)
    *   Edges in P3: (M,L), (L,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G), (G,H), (H,B)

    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect M and B is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set of size 3:**

    Consider the cut set: **[(M, E), (M, J), (L, E)]**

    *   Removing node B will break all paths that start L -> E -> ...

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, will completely disconnect H from B.

First, I will identify all distinct paths between H and B. To do this systematically, I'll categorize paths based on the first edge taken from H.

**Paths from H to B:**

1.  **Paths starting with (H, C):**
    *   H - C - B

2.  **Paths starting with (H, I):**
    *   H - I - J - M - O - B
    *   H - I - J - D - P - O - B
    *   H - I - J - D - P - O - N - B
    *   H - I - P - O - B
    *   H - I - P - O - N - B

3.  **Paths starting with (H, G):**
    *   H - G - F - E - B
    *   H - G - X - K - F - E - B

4.  **Paths starting with (H, X):**
    *   H - X - K - F - E - B
    *   H - X - G - F - E - B

To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I can look for edge-disjoint paths. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed.

Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
*   **Path 1:** H - C - B (Edges: (H, C), (C, B))
*   **Path 2:** H - I - P - O - B (Edges: (H, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, B))
*   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - B (Edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B))

Upon inspection, these three paths do not share any common edges. Therefore, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of exactly 3 edges that disconnects H and B. I will choose edges strategically to cover all identified path categories.

Consider the following set of three edges: **[(H, C), (H, I), (E, B)]**

Let's verify if removing these three edges breaks all paths:

1.  **Removing (H, C):** This directly breaks the path H - C - B. All paths in category 1 are now disconnected.
2.  **Removing (H, I):** This breaks all paths that start with the edge (H, I). This covers all paths listed in category 2.
3.  **Removing (E, B):** This breaks all paths that end with the edge (E, B).
    *   For paths in category 3 (starting with H-G): Both H - G - F - E - B and H - G - X - K - F - E - B are broken by removing (E, B).
    *   For paths in category 4 (starting with H-X): Both H - X - K - F - E - B and H - X - G - F - E - B are broken by removing (E, B).

Since every path from H to B must start with one of (H, C), (H, I), (H, G), or (H, X), and our chosen cut set addresses all these possibilities, H and B are completely disconnected.

Because we found a cut set of size 3, and we established that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, C), (H, I), (E, B)]

    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least 3.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from M to B:

        Edges: `(M, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, B)`

    *   From Path 1: `(M, E)`, `(E, H)`, `(H, B)`
    *   From Path 2: `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, B)`
    *   From Path 3: `(M, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, B)`

    All 11 edges listed above are distinct. No edge is shared between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.
    We previously found a cut set of size 3: `[(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)]`. This set successfully cuts all paths originating from M, thus disconnecting M from B.

    Node E is connected to L, M, F, and B.
    Therefore, any path from E to J must start by traversing one of these four edges: (E,L), (E,M), (E,F), or (E,B).

    If we remove all edges connected to E, E would be completely isolated, and thus disconnected from J. This would require removing 4 edges: (E,L), (E,M), (E,F), (E,B). This is a valid cut set, but we need to find the *minimum* number of edges.

3.  **Test for a cut set of size 1 or 2:**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect E from J, as there are multiple independent paths. For example, if (E,F) is removed, paths like E-B-H-I-J still exist.
    *   **Size 2:** If we remove any two edges connected to E, say (E,F) and (E,B), E would still be connected to L and M.

        *   Path E-L-M-F-K-J would still exist (as (E,L) and (E,M) are not removed).
        Therefore, 2 edges are not sufficient.

4.  **Test for a cut set of size 3:**

    *   **Edge (E,F) removed:** All paths starting E-F-... are broken.
    *   **Edge (E,B) removed:** All paths starting E-B-... are broken.
    *   **Edge (E,M) removed:** All paths starting E-M-... are broken.

    *   Node L is connected only to E and M.
    *   Since (E,M) has been removed, L is now only connected to E.
    *   Therefore, L cannot provide a path from E to any other node in the graph, including J.

    This means that after removing [(E,F), (E,B), (E,M)], node E is effectively disconnected from the rest of the graph (except for L, which itself is isolated from J's side of the graph).
    This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects E from J.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 2 edges are not sufficient and 3 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    Node G is directly connected to X, K, F, and H.
    Nodes X, K, F are also interconnected: (X, K), (X, F), (K, F).
    This means G, X, K, F form a tightly connected sub-graph (a clique of 4 nodes, or almost, G is connected to all of them, and they are connected to each other).

2.  **Identify "exit points" from the G-cluster:**
    To reach node N, any path originating from G must first leave this {G, X, K, F} cluster.
    Let's examine the edges connecting this cluster to the rest of the graph:
    *   (G, H): Connects G to H. H is part of the larger graph leading towards N.
    *   (F, E): Connects F to E. E is part of the larger graph leading towards N.
    *   All other edges from G (G,X), (G,K), (G,F) lead to nodes within the {G, X, K, F} cluster.
    *   All other edges from X (X,K), (X,F) lead to nodes within the {G, X, K, F} cluster.
    *   All other edges from K (K,F) lead to nodes within the {G, X, K, F} cluster.

    Therefore, any path from G to N *must* traverse either the edge (G, H) or the edge (F, E) to leave the {G, X, K, F} cluster and proceed towards N.

3.  **Formulate a cut set:**
    To disconnect G from N, we must remove both of these "exit" edges: (G, H) and (F, E).
    *   If (G, H) is removed, G can still reach F (via G-F, G-X-F, or G-K-F), and from F, it can reach E via (F, E). From E, it can continue towards N.
    *   If (F, E) is removed, G can still reach H via (G, H), and from H, it can continue towards N.
    *   If both (G, H) and (F, E) are removed, then G (and its connected nodes X, K, F) cannot reach H and cannot reach E. Since H and E are the only gateways from the {G, X, K, F} cluster to the rest of the graph, G becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

4.  **Determine minimality:**
    *   A cut of size 1 is not possible, as removing either (G, H) or (F, E) alone leaves an alternative path (as explained above).
    *   The proposed cut set [(G, H), (F, E)] has a size of 2.
    *   Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, a cut of size 2 is the minimum.

5.  **Verify against all paths:**
    Any path from G to N will inevitably use either (G, H) or (F, E) as its first step out of the {G, X, K, F} component. Removing both edges ensures that no such path can be formed.

    *   Node F is connected to: K, X, G, E.

    Let's define a set of nodes S that contains F and its immediate neighborhood, and a set T that contains J and its neighborhood.
    Consider the set S = {F, K, X, G, E, L, M}. These are nodes that are either F, or directly connected to F, or connected to F's direct neighbors in a way that keeps them "on F's side" without crossing into J's main area.

        Therefore, removing these two edges does not disconnect F from J.

    Since removing any two edges from the identified set of three leaves at least one path, all three edges are necessary to disconnect F from J. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

2.  **Consider a cut set by removing all edges connected to J:**
    If we remove all four edges (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), and (J, I), node J becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist between E and J.
    This constitutes a valid cut set of size 4.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (size 3):**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, I need to show that no set of 3 edges can disconnect E from J.

    Let's consider the four direct connections to J: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).
    If we remove any three of these four edges, one edge connecting J to one of its neighbors (M, D, P, or I) will remain. We must then check if E can still reach that remaining neighbor.

    *   **Case 1: Remove (J, M), (J, D), (J, P).**

        Path E-B-C-I exists. Therefore, the path E-B-C-I-J still exists.
        This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Case 2: Remove (J, M), (J, D), (J, I).**

        Path E-B-C-O-P exists. Therefore, the path E-B-C-O-P-J still exists.
        This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Case 3: Remove (J, M), (J, P), (J, I).**
        The edge (J, D) remains. We need to check if there is a path from E to D.
        Path E-M-D exists. Therefore, the path E-M-D-J still exists.
        This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Case 4: Remove (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).**
        The edge (J, M) remains. We need to check if there is a path from E to M.
        Path E-M exists. Therefore, the path E-M-J still exists.
        This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    Since removing any 3 of the 4 edges directly connected to J leaves at least one path from E to J, it is impossible to disconnect E from J by removing only 3 edges that are direct connections to J.

    Now, let's consider if a cut of 3 edges could exist by removing edges *not* directly connected to J.
    Let's define two sets of nodes:
    *   Set S (E-side): {E, L, F, X, K, G, H, B, N, C, Y, O}
    *   Set T (J-side): {J, D, P, I, M}

    (E, M) - E in S, M in T
    (L, M) - L in S, M in T
    (O, P) - O in S, P in T
    (C, I) - C in S, I in T
    (H, I) - H in S, I in T
    There are 5 such edges. Removing all of them would disconnect E from J, but this is a cut of size 5, which is larger than 4.

    Since a cut of size 4 has been found, and no cut of size 3 could be identified through systematic exploration, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

    *   Therefore, {P, I} is not a cut set.

    *   However, paths like E - M - J, E - L - M - J, E - P - J, and E - P - I - J still exist.
    *   Therefore, {B, I} is not a cut set.

*   All paths through M (E-M-J, E-L-M-J) are broken by M.

*   The only remaining neighbor of E is L. The path E-L-M-J is broken because M is removed.
After removing M, P, and B, there are no remaining paths from E to J.

*   All paths through M are broken by M.
*   All paths through P are broken by P.
*   All paths through I (E-B-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-C-H-I-J, E-P-I-J) are broken by I.

Since I have systematically checked that no set of 1 or 2 nodes can disconnect E and J, and I have found a set of 3 nodes that does, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from J. I cannot remove E or J.

I will systematically identify all paths from E to J and look for common nodes that must be traversed.

Let's list the direct neighbors of E: M, L, F, B.
Let's list the direct neighbors of J: M, P, I, D.

Notice that node D is connected to J and P. Any path from E to J that might involve D would have to go through P (e.g., J-D-P). Therefore, if P is removed, any path through D is also effectively blocked from reaching J via P. D is not a critical node for the cut set itself.

Now, let's consider the "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph. All paths from E to J must pass through one of J's direct neighbors (excluding J itself). These neighbors are M, P, and I (as D's connection to J is covered by P).

Let's analyze if removing the set of nodes {M, P, I} would disconnect E and J:

1.  **Paths through M:**
    *   E - M - J: This path is broken by removing M.
    *   E - L - M - J: This path is broken by removing M.

2.  **Paths through F:**
    *   E - F - X - K - G - H - I - J: This path is broken by removing I.
    *   E - F - K - G - H - I - J: This path is broken by removing I.
    *   E - F - K - X - G - H - I - J: This path is broken by removing I.
    All paths starting E -> F eventually lead to I before reaching J.

3.  **Paths through B:**
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J: This path is broken by removing P.
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J: This path is broken by removing P.
    *   E - B - C - O - P - J: This path is broken by removing P.
    *   E - B - C - I - J: This path is broken by removing I.
    *   E - B - H - I - J: This path is broken by removing I.
    All paths starting E -> B eventually lead to P or I before reaching J.

Since every path from E to J must pass through at least one of M, P, or I, removing these three nodes will successfully disconnect E from J. This gives a cut set of size 3: [M, P, I].

Now, I need to verify that this is the *minimum* number of nodes. I will check if removing any two of these nodes would be sufficient:

*   **Attempt to remove 2 nodes: {M, P}**
    *   Paths E - M - J and E - L - M - J are broken (by M).
    *   Paths E - B - ... - P - J are broken (by P).

1.  **Identify the immediate neighbors of G:** {X, K, F, H}.
2.  **Identify the immediate neighbors of I:** {E, H, B, C, J, P}.

I will analyze all possible paths from G to I. Any path from G to I must start by traversing an edge connected to G. Let's consider the "bottleneck" edges that connect the "G-side" of the graph to the "I-side".

Let's define a set of nodes `S_G = {G, X, K, F}`. These are G and its direct neighbors, excluding H for a moment, to see if there's a clear separation.
The edges connecting `S_G` to the rest of the graph are:
*   (G, H)
*   (F, E)
*   (X, K) - This edge is internal to `S_G` (X and K are in `S_G`).
*   (X, F) - This edge is internal to `S_G`.
*   (K, F) - This edge is internal to `S_G`.

This means that any path originating from G and aiming to reach I must eventually leave the cluster `S_G`. The only edges that allow a path to leave `S_G` are (G, H) and (F, E).

Let's verify this systematically:
*   **Path starting G-H:** If a path starts with G-H, it uses the edge (G, H). If we remove (G, H), this type of path is broken.
*   **Path starting G-F:** From F, a path can go to X, K, or E.
    *   If it goes to X or K (e.g., G-F-X or G-F-K), it remains within the `S_G` cluster. To eventually reach I, it must still leave `S_G`. The only edge available to leave `S_G` (other than (G, H)) is (F, E). So, any path starting G-F and not using (G, H) must eventually use (F, E).
*   **Path starting G-X:** From X, a path can go to K or F.
    *   If it goes to K or F (e.g., G-X-K or G-X-F), it remains within the `S_G` cluster. Similar to the G-F case, it must eventually use (F, E) to leave `S_G` and reach I.
*   **Path starting G-K:** From K, a path can go to X or F.
    *   If it goes to X or F (e.g., G-K-X or G-K-F), it remains within the `S_G` cluster. Similar to the G-F and G-X cases, it must eventually use (F, E) to leave `S_G` and reach I.

Therefore, every single path from G to I must traverse either the edge (G, H) or the edge (F, E).

To disconnect G from I, we must remove both of these edges.
*   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that directly use H as the first step from G.
*   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths that go through F (and potentially X or K) and then E to reach I.

Let's confirm that removing these two edges completely disconnects G from I:
After removing (G, H) and (F, E):
*   G is connected to X, K, F.
*   F is connected to G, X, K. (F is no longer connected to E).
*   X is connected to G, K, F.
*   K is connected to G, X, F.
The component containing G is {G, X, K, F}.
The node I is connected to {H, B, C, J, P, E}.
There are no remaining edges connecting {G, X, K, F} to {H, B, C, J, P, E}. Thus, G is disconnected from I.

This cut set has 2 edges.
Could there be a cut set of size 1?
No, because if we remove only (G, H), paths like G-F-E-I still exist.
If we remove only (F, E), paths like G-H-I still exist.
Therefore, a minimum of 2 edges must be removed.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

1.  **Identify direct connections from H:**
    Node H is directly connected to G, I, and B. These three connections represent the initial "branches" for any path originating from H.

2.  **Identify distinct paths:**
    Let's find at least three edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, we need to remove at least *k* edges to disconnect the nodes.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** H - B
        This is a direct path.

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** H - G - F - E - B
        This path uses edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B).

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** H - I - C - B
        This path uses edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, B).

3.  **Check for edge-disjointness:**
    Let's examine the edges used in these three paths:
    *   P1: {(H, B)}
    *   P2: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B)}
    *   P3: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}

    All edges in P1 are distinct from edges in P2 and P3.
    All edges in P2 are distinct from edges in P1 and P3.
    All edges in P3 are distinct from edges in P1 and P2.
    Therefore, these three paths (P1, P2, P3) are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from B.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from B is to remove all edges directly connected to H that lead to B or to parts of the graph that can reach B. These are:
    *   (H, B) - cuts P1
    *   (H, G) - cuts P2 (and all other paths starting H-G-...)
    *   (H, I) - cuts P3 (and all other paths starting H-I-...)

    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node B. This cut set has a size of 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (requiring at least 3 edges to be removed) and found a cut set of 3 edges that achieves the disconnection, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and O. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and O. I cannot remove H or O.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (the vertex-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

First, I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. Vertex-disjoint means that the paths share no intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and O).

Let's find the first path:
1.  **Path 1: H - B - N - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {B, N}

Now, I need to find a second path that does not use B or N.
2.  **Path 2: H - I - C - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {I, C}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {B, N} and {I, C} are disjoint sets.

Now, I need to find a third path that does not use B, N, I, or C.
3.  **Path 3: H - E - M - J - P - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {E, M, J, P}
    Let's check for disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2:
    -   {E, M, J, P} is disjoint from {B, N}.
    -   {E, M, J, P} is disjoint from {I, C}.
    Therefore, Path 3 is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

I have successfully identified three vertex-disjoint paths between H and O:
*   P1: H -> B -> N -> O (Intermediate nodes: B, N)
*   P2: H -> I -> C -> O (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
*   P3: H -> E -> M -> J -> P -> O (Intermediate nodes: E, M, J, P)

Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and O is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of size 3 and verify that it disconnects H and O.
Consider the nodes B, I, and E. These are the first intermediate nodes in each of the three disjoint paths identified above.

Let's verify if removing nodes {B, I, E} disconnects H from O:
*   If B is removed, all paths starting with H-B-... are broken (e.g., H-B-N-O, H-B-C-O).
*   If I is removed, all paths starting with H-I-... are broken (e.g., H-I-C-O, H-I-P-O, H-I-J-P-O). Also, paths like H-E-I-... are broken.
*   If E is removed, all paths starting with H-E-... are broken (e.g., H-E-M-J-P-O, H-E-I-C-O). Additionally, any paths from H through F or G that rely on E are also broken (e.g., H-F-E-..., H-G-F-E-...).

After removing B, I, and E, let's check if any path remains from H to O:
*   H's remaining direct neighbors are F and G.
*   F is connected to E (which is removed), G, K, X. None of these (G, K, X) lead to O without passing through B, I, or E.
*   G is connected to F, X, K. None of these (F, X, K) lead to O without passing through B, I, or E.

Therefore, removing the nodes {B, I, E} successfully disconnects H from O.
Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and J.

First, I will identify several edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1: E - M - J**
    Edges: (E, M), (M, J)

2.  **Path 2: E - P - J**
    Edges: (E, P), (P, J)

3.  **Path 3: E - B - H - I - J**
    Edges: (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)

Let's check if these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
*   Edges in Path 1: {(E, M), (M, J)}
*   Edges in Path 2: {(E, P), (P, J)}
*   Edges in Path 3: {(E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)}

Comparing these sets, there are no common edges. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.
According to Menger's theorem, if there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, then at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. This establishes a lower bound of 3 for the minimum cut.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and J.
Consider the edges directly connected to node J: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).
If we remove the edges (J, M), (J, P), and (J, I), let's analyze the connectivity of J.
After removing these three edges, node J is only connected to node D.
Now, let's check node D. Node D is only connected to J.
Since D is only connected to J, and J is now only connected to D (from the perspective of the rest of the graph), J becomes completely isolated from all other nodes in the graph, including E.

Let's verify this cut set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)].
1.  Removing (J, M) breaks Path 1 (E - M - J).
2.  Removing (J, P) breaks Path 2 (E - P - J).
3.  Removing (J, I) breaks Path 3 (E - B - H - I - J), as well as any other path that would reach J via I (e.g., E - B - C - I - J, E - P - F - G - H - I - J).

Since J is now only connected to D, and D is only connected to J, there is no way for E to reach J. All paths are broken.
This cut set consists of 3 edges. Since we established a lower bound of 3 edges, this cut set is indeed minimal.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(I, J), (M, J), (P, J)]

    *   The size of this cut set is 2.

    *   F has four direct neighbors: K, X, G, E.

    *   **Category 1: Paths through E.**

        *   Path 1.2: F - E - B - ... (These paths continue through B, then branch out to H, C, or N, eventually reaching I or P, and then J). Examples: F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-I-J, F-E-B-N-O-P-J.
    *   **Category 2: Paths through K, X, or G.**
        *   All paths starting from F through K, X, or G eventually converge at node H to proceed towards J. Examples: F-K-X-G-H-I-J, F-X-G-H-B-C-I-J, F-G-H-I-P-J.

    *   If I remove **E**: All paths in Category 1 (F-E-...) are broken. However, paths in Category 2 (F-K/X/G-H-...) still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove **H**: All paths in Category 2 (F-K/X/G-H-...) are broken. However, paths like F-E-M-J and F-E-B-C-I-J still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   Similarly, removing any other single node (K, X, G, B, C, I, P, N, O, D, L, Y) will leave at least one path intact. For example, removing B would leave F-E-M-J and F-G-H-I-J. Removing I would leave F-E-M-J and F-G-H-I-P-J.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Test for Two Node Cut:**
    *   Consider the two main "entry points" from F to the rest of the graph: E and the cluster {K, X, G}.
    *   If we remove node **E**:
        *   All paths in Category 1 (F-E-M-J and F-E-B-...) are broken.
        *   Now, all remaining paths from F to J must go through K, X, or G.
    *   From K, X, or G, the only way to reach the "right side" of the graph (where J is located) is through node **H** (via the G-H connection).
    *   Therefore, if we also remove node **H**:
        *   All paths from F through K, X, or G are broken because the G-H connection is severed.
    *   With both E and H removed, F is completely disconnected from J.

6.  **Verify the Cut Set [E, H]:**
    *   Removing E breaks: F-E-M-J, F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-I-J, F-E-B-N-O-P-J, and all variations.
    *   After E is removed, any remaining path from F must start F-K-..., F-X-..., or F-G-...
    *   Removing H breaks: F-K-X-G-H-I-J, F-X-G-H-B-C-I-J, F-G-H-I-P-J, and all variations.
    *   Since all paths are covered by the removal of E or H, the set [E, H] successfully disconnects F from J.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and a two-node removal ([E, H]) is sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Next, let's list the direct neighbors of B: E, G, H, C, N.

**Category 1: Paths starting M -> L**
*   M -> L -> E -> B (This path includes E)
*   M -> L -> E -> G -> B (This path includes E and G)
    *   All paths starting with M -> L must pass through E.

**Category 2: Paths starting M -> E (direct connection)**
*   M -> E -> B (This path includes E)
*   M -> E -> G -> B (This path includes E and G)
    *   All paths starting with M -> E must pass through E.

**Category 3: Paths starting M -> F**
*   M -> F -> G -> B (This path includes G)
*   M -> F -> X -> K -> G -> B (This path includes G)
    *   All paths starting with M -> F must pass through G.

**Category 4: Paths starting M -> J**
*   M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B (This path includes P)
*   M -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B (This path includes P)
*   M -> J -> P -> I -> C -> B (This path includes P)
*   M -> J -> P -> I -> H -> B (This path includes P)
    *   All paths starting with M -> J must pass through P.

From this analysis, we can observe that:
*   Any path from M that goes through L or directly through E *must* pass through node E.
*   Any path from M that goes through F *must* pass through node G.
*   Any path from M that goes through J *must* pass through node P.

Therefore, to disconnect M from B, we must remove nodes E, G, and P. This set of nodes {E, G, P} forms a cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

Now, I need to verify if a smaller cut set is possible (i.e., 1 or 2 nodes).

**Attempting to disconnect with 1 node:**
*   If I remove E: Paths through F (e.g., M -> F -> G -> B) and paths through J (e.g., M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B) would still exist.
*   If I remove G: Paths through E (e.g., M -> E -> B) and paths through J (e.g., M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B) would still exist.
*   If I remove P: Paths through E (e.g., M -> E -> B) and paths through F (e.g., M -> F -> G -> B) would still exist.
*   Removing any other single node (L, F, J, X, K, H, I, C, O, N, Y, D) would leave multiple paths intact.
Thus, a single node removal is not sufficient.

**Attempting to disconnect with 2 nodes:**
*   If I remove {E, G}: Paths through J (e.g., M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B) would still exist.
*   If I remove {E, P}: Paths through F (e.g., M -> F -> G -> B) would still exist.
*   If I remove {G, P}: Paths through E (e.g., M -> E -> B) and (M -> L -> E -> B) would still exist.
*   Any other combination of two nodes would also leave at least one path. For example, removing {L, F} would still leave paths through E (M -> E -> B) and J (M -> J -> P -> ... -> B).

Since removing any 1 or 2 nodes does not disconnect M from B, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. The cut set is {E, G, P}.

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G from I.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: K, X, F, E, H.
    *   Node I is connected to: H, E, J, P, C, O.

2.  **Analyze the structure of paths between G and I:**
    Let's consider how paths can flow from G to I.
    *   From G, a path can go directly to E (G-E-...) or H (G-H-...).
    *   From G, a path can also go to F (G-F-...). From F, the only connection that leads towards the "I-side" of the graph is to E (F-E). So, any path starting G-F-... must continue G-F-E-...

    This means that *all paths from G to I must pass through either node E or node H*. This is a crucial observation.

3.  **Identify potential cut edges based on the bottleneck nodes (E and H):**
    Since all paths must pass through E or H, we need to cut all connections that allow G (or its local cluster) to reach E or H.
    Let's define a "G-side" component as the set of nodes {G, K, X, F} and an "I-side" component as the rest of the graph containing I. We need to find the edges that connect these two components.

    *   Edges directly from G to E or H:
        *   (G, E)
        *   (G, H)
    *   Edges from nodes connected to G (like F) to E or H:
        *   F is connected to G. From F, the only edge leading to E or H (and thus towards I) is (F, E).

    Therefore, the set of edges that connect the {G, K, X, F} cluster to the rest of the graph (which contains I) is:
    *   (G, E)
    *   (G, H)
    *   (F, E)

4.  **Test the identified cut set:**
    If we remove these three edges: [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]:
    *   (G, E) is removed, so G cannot directly reach E.
    *   (G, H) is removed, so G cannot directly reach H.
    *   (F, E) is removed. Since F is only connected to E (among the nodes leading to I), G cannot reach E via F anymore.
    After removing these three edges, G is only connected to K and X. Neither K nor X have any connections to E, H, or any other node that could lead to I. Thus, G is completely disconnected from I.
    This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Verify minimality (can we do it with fewer edges?):**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?** No. If we remove any single edge from the set [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)], the other two paths remain. For example, removing (G, E) leaves paths like G-H-I and G-F-E-I.
    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?** No.
        *   If we remove (G, E) and (G, H): The path G-F-E-I still exists.
        *   If we remove (G, E) and (F, E): The path G-H-I still exists.
        *   If we remove (G, H) and (F, E): The path G-E-I still exists.
    Since removing any two edges from this set of three leaves at least one path open, a cut of size 2 is not possible.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]

    All paths originating from L must start by traversing either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E). There are no other edges connected to L.

3.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to L:**
    *   If I remove the edge (L, M), L can still reach J via paths starting with L-E-..., for example, L-E-B-C-I-J or L-E-M-J (if (M,J) is not cut).
    *   If I remove the edge (L, E), L can still reach J via the path L-M-J.
    *   Since removing a single edge (either (L, M) or (L, E)) does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Propose a cut set of size 2:**
    Consider removing both edges directly connected to L: (L, M) and (L, E).
    *   Removing (L, M) severs all paths that start L-M-...
    *   Removing (L, E) severs all paths that start L-E-...
    *   Since L has no other connections, removing these two edges completely isolates L from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from L to J.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    As established in step 3, removing any single edge is insufficient to disconnect L and J. Therefore, a minimum of 2 edges is required. The proposed cut set of 2 edges successfully disconnects L from J.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 2, and one such cut set is [(L, M), (L, E)].

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, M), (L, E)]

Image name: PG_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are O and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect O and H. I cannot remove O or H.

I will systematically identify all distinct paths from O to H and then find a minimal set of nodes whose removal severs all these paths.

First, let's list the direct neighbors of O: P, Y, N, C, M.
Next, let's list the direct neighbors of H: I, C, X, G.

Notice that node C is a common neighbor to both O and H. This means there is a direct path:
1.  **O - C - H**

Now, let's consider paths that do not use C. These paths must go from O to one of H's other neighbors (I, X, G).

**Paths from O to H via I:**
All paths from O to I must pass through node J. Let's verify:
*   O is connected to P, M.
*   P is connected to D. D is connected to J. So, O - P - D - J - I - H is a path.
*   M is connected to J. So, O - M - J - I - H is a path.
*   Other paths to J: O - Y - N - B - E - L - M - J - I - H and O - N - B - E - L - M - J - I - H.
Therefore, all paths from O to H that pass through I must traverse node J. Removing J would disconnect O from I.

**Paths from O to H via X:**
All paths from O to X must pass through node E. Let's verify:
*   Paths from O to X:
    *   O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - H
    *   O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - K - X - H
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - X - H
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - K - X - H
    *   O - N - B - E - F - X - H
    *   O - N - B - E - F - K - X - H
    *   O - M - L - E - F - X - H
    *   O - M - L - E - F - K - X - H
All these paths clearly show that E is a bottleneck for reaching X from O. Removing E would disconnect O from X.

**Paths from O to H via G:**
Similarly, all paths from O to G must also pass through node E. Let's verify:
*   Paths from O to G:
    *   O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - G - H
    *   O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - G - H
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - K - G - H
    *   O - N - B - E - F - G - H
    *   O - N - B - E - F - K - G - H
    *   O - M - L - E - F - G - H
    *   O - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
Again, all these paths show that E is a bottleneck for reaching G from O. Removing E would disconnect O from G.

Based on this analysis, we have identified three critical nodes that act as bottlenecks for all paths between O and H:
1.  **C**: Breaks the direct path O - C - H.
2.  **J**: Breaks all paths from O to I (which then connects to H).
3.  **E**: Breaks all paths from O to X and G (which then connect to H).

If we remove the set of nodes {C, J, E}:
*   Removing C breaks the path O-C-H.
*   Removing J breaks all paths that go through I to H.
*   Removing E breaks all paths that go through X or G to H.

Therefore, removing {C, J, E} will completely disconnect O from H. This is a cut set of size 3.

Now, let's verify if a smaller set (1 or 2 nodes) can achieve disconnection.
*   **1 node removal:**
    *   If we remove C, paths like O-M-J-I-H and O-N-B-E-F-X-H still exist.
    *   If we remove J, paths like O-C-H and O-N-B-E-F-X-H still exist.
    *   If we remove E, paths like O-C-H and O-M-J-I-H still exist.
    No single node can disconnect O and H.

*   **2 nodes removal:**
    *   If we remove {C, J}: The path O-N-B-E-F-X-H (and other paths through E to X or G) still exists.
    *   If we remove {C, E}: The path O-M-J-I-H (and other paths through J to I) still exists.
    *   If we remove {J, E}: The path O-C-H still exists.
    No combination of two nodes from our identified critical set can disconnect O and H. Since these three nodes cover all distinct "bundles" of paths, any other pair of nodes would also leave at least one path intact.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, J, E]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'I']}

    *   Paths originating from H via G, then through F, M, J, and P:
        *   H - G - F - M - J - P - O
    *   Paths originating from H via F, then through M, J, and P:
        *   H - F - M - J - P - O
    All these paths rely on node P to connect to O.

To disconnect H and O, I must break all three of these distinct path groups.

*   To break **Path Group 1 (H-I-O)**, node I must be removed.
*   To break **Path Group 2 (through B)**, node B must be removed. Removing B severs all connections that pass through it to C or N, which are direct neighbors of O. (Removing C and N would also work, but that's 2 nodes, whereas B is 1).
*   To break **Path Group 3 (through F-M-J-P)**, node P must be removed. Removing P severs the connection to O for all paths coming through M and J. (Removing F, M, or J would also work, but P is the closest to O and ensures all paths through this channel are cut).

Therefore, a set of three nodes {I, B, P} would disconnect H and O.

Now, I need to verify if a smaller set of nodes (1 or 2) can achieve disconnection.

**Checking for a 1-node cut set:**
*   Removing I: Paths through B (e.g., H-B-C-O) and paths through P (e.g., H-G-F-M-J-P-O) would remain.
*   Removing B: Paths through I (H-I-O) and paths through P (e.g., H-G-F-M-J-P-O) would remain.
*   Removing P: Paths through I (H-I-O) and paths through B (e.g., H-B-C-O) would remain.
*   Removing any other single node (e.g., F, G, C, N, M, J, E, L, K, X, Y, D) would leave multiple path groups intact.
Thus, a single node removal is insufficient.

**Checking for a 2-node cut set:**
*   Removing {I, B}: Path Group 3 (through P) remains. For example, H-G-F-M-J-P-O still exists.
*   Removing {I, P}: Path Group 2 (through B) remains. For example, H-B-C-O still exists.
*   Removing {B, P}: Path Group 1 (through I) remains. For example, H-I-O still exists.
*   Any other pair of nodes would also leave at least one path group intact. For example, removing {F, E} would still leave H-I-O, H-B-C-O, H-B-N-O, H-G-F-M-J-P-O.
Thus, a two-node removal is insufficient.

Since a 1-node or 2-node cut set is not sufficient, and a 3-node cut set {I, B, P} successfully disconnects H and O, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [I, B, P]

2.  **Strategy - Menger's Theorem:** I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. First, I will find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths from H to E.

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths:**
    I will systematically search for paths from H to E, ensuring that each new path does not share any edges with previously found paths.

    *   **Path 1:** H - G - K - F - E
        Edges used: (H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E)

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - J - M - L - E
        Edges used: (H,I), (I,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E)
        (Verify: No edges from Path 2 are present in Path 1. These are edge-disjoint.)

    *   **Path 3:** H - C - N - B - E
        Edges used: (H,C), (C,N), (N,B), (B,E)
        (Verify: No edges from Path 3 are present in Path 1 or Path 2. These are edge-disjoint.)

4.  **Confirm Edge-Disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges from the three paths:
    *   Path 1: {(H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E)}
    *   Path 2: {(H,I), (I,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E)}
    *   Path 3: {(H,C), (C,N), (N,B), (B,E)}
    All edges listed are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and E is at least 3.

6.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** To confirm this minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and E. A simple and effective strategy is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    The edges directly connected to node E are:
    *   (E, B)
    *   (E, L)
    *   (E, F)

7.  **Verify the Cut Set:** If these three edges [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)] are removed, node E becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from H to E. This cut set has a size of 3.

8.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths (implying a minimum cut of at least 3) and identified a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects H and E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the black nodes I and E, without removing I or E themselves. This is equivalent to finding the minimum vertex cut between I and E. By Menger's Theorem, the size of the minimum vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths from I to E:

1.  **Path 1 (P1): I - J - M - E**
    *   Nodes used in this path (excluding I and E): {J, M}

2.  **Path 2 (P2): I - C - B - E**
    *   Nodes used in this path (excluding I and E): {C, B}
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from P1, as {J, M} and {C, B} are distinct sets of nodes.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): I - H - G - F - E**
    *   Nodes used in this path (excluding I and E): {H, G, F}
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from P1 and P2, as {H, G, F} is distinct from {J, M} and {C, B}.

Now, let's try to find a fourth path that is vertex-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.
The nodes used so far are {J, M, C, B, H, G, F}.
The remaining unvisited neighbors of I are O.
Let's try to construct a path from I through O to E without using any of the nodes in {J, M, C, B, H, G, F}.

From O, its neighbors are P, N, Y, C.
*   O -> C: C is already used in P2. Cannot use.
*   O -> N: N's neighbors are B, Y, O. B is used in P2. Y's neighbors are N, O. This path would lead to N or Y, but then to B, which is already used.
*   O -> Y: Y's neighbors are N, O. N leads to B (used).
*   O -> P: P's neighbors are D, O, J. J is used in P1. D's neighbors are J, P. J is used in P1.

It appears that any path starting from I-O eventually uses one of the nodes already part of P1, P2, or P3. For example, I-O-N-B-E uses B (from P2). I-O-P-D-J-M-E uses J and M (from P1).

Therefore, the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between I and E is 3.
According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect I and E is 3.

Now, we need to find a cut set of size 3.
Consider the nodes directly connected to E that are not E itself: B, F, L, M. L is a leaf node connected only to E, so removing L only disconnects L from E, not I from E.
The critical nodes connecting to E are B, F, M. Let's test removing these three nodes: [B, F, M].

1.  **Paths through M:** Any path using M (e.g., I-J-M-E) is broken because M is removed.
2.  **Paths through B:** Any path using B (e.g., I-C-B-E, I-O-N-B-E) is broken because B is removed.
3.  **Paths through F:** Any path using F (e.g., I-H-G-F-E) is broken because F is removed.

Since all paths from I to E must pass through at least one of B, F, or M to reach E (as these are the only non-leaf neighbors of E that can be reached from I), removing these three nodes will disconnect I from E.

Each node in the cut set [B, F, M] is necessary:
*   If B is not removed, paths like I-C-B-E would remain.
*   If F is not removed, paths like I-H-G-F-E would remain.
*   If M is not removed, paths like I-J-M-E would remain.

Thus, [B, F, M] is a minimal cut set of size 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, M]

    The edges connected to B are: (B, H), (B, E), (B, I), (B, C), (B, N).
    There are 5 such edges. If we remove all of them, B is disconnected from H. This is a valid cut set of size 5.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 4 edges) is possible.**
    To prove that 5 is the minimum, we need to show that removing any 4 edges will *not* disconnect H from B.
    Let's consider the set of all 5 edges incident to B: S_B = {(B, H), (B, E), (B, I), (B, C), (B, N)}.
    If we remove any 4 edges from S_B, exactly one edge (B, X) will remain, where X is one of H, E, I, C, N. For H and B to remain connected, H must be able to reach X without using any of the removed edges.

    *   **Case 1: (B, H) remains.**
        Path: H - B. (H is directly connected to B).

        Path: H - I - C - B. (H is connected to I via (H, I), I is connected to C via (I, C), and C is connected to B via (C, B)).
    *   **Case 5: (B, N) remains.**
        Path: H - I - C - N - B. (H is connected to I via (H, I), I is connected to C via (I, C), C is connected to N via (C, N), and N is connected to B via (N, B)).

    In all 5 scenarios, if only 4 edges incident to B are removed, a path from H to B still exists. This demonstrates that removing 4 edges is not sufficient to disconnect H from B.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 4 edges are not enough, and 5 edges (all edges incident to B) are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 5.

Edges to remove: 5, Cut Set: [(B, H), (B, E), (B, I), (B, C), (B, N)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J (black) and F (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J from F.

1.  **Identify all possible paths from J to F:**
    Paths from J to F can be broadly categorized based on the nodes they pass through to reach F. The nodes directly connected to F are E, K, X, G, H. Therefore, any path from J to F must eventually pass through one of these five nodes and then use an edge to F.

2.  **Analyze the structure of connections to F:**
    *   **Paths through E to F:** All paths that use the edge (E,F). Node E is connected to B and L.
    *   **Paths through K, X, G, H to F:** These nodes form a cluster.
        *   H is connected to I, C, X, G, F.
        *   X is connected to H, K, G, F.
        *   G is connected to H, X, K, F.
        *   K is connected to X, G, F.
        Notice that to reach K, X, or G from J, one must first pass through H. For example, to reach K, you can go via X or G. Both X and G are connected to H. So, if H is disconnected from J, then K, X, and G are also effectively disconnected from J (in terms of reaching F through them).

3.  **Identify potential bottleneck edges:**
    Based on the analysis above, we can identify two main "channels" through which paths flow from J to F:
    *   **Channel 1: The "E-channel"**: All paths that eventually reach F via node E.
    *   **Channel 2: The "H-cluster channel"**: All paths that eventually reach F via nodes H, K, X, or G.

4.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect J from F, we need to cut all paths in both channels.

    *   **Cutting the "E-channel"**: The simplest way to cut all paths that use E to reach F is to remove the edge **(E,F)**. This single edge removal breaks all paths like J-...-E-F.

    *   **Cutting the "H-cluster channel"**: All paths from J to F that go through H, K, X, or G must first reach H from J. The connections from J to H are:
        *   J - I - H
        *   J - I - C - H
        To cut all these paths, we need to remove the edges that connect H to the "J-side" of the graph. These edges are **(I,H)** and **(C,H)**.
        *   Removing **(I,H)** breaks all paths that go J-I-H-...-F.
        *   Removing **(C,H)** breaks all paths that go J-I-C-H-...-F.
        Once (I,H) and (C,H) are removed, node H (and consequently K, X, G, which rely on H for connection to the J-side) becomes completely disconnected from J. Therefore, all paths from J to F that pass through H, K, X, or G are broken.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    The proposed cut set is **[(E,F), (I,H), (C,H)]**.
    *   Removing (E,F) ensures no path can reach F via E.
    *   Removing (I,H) ensures no path can reach H via I directly.
    *   Removing (C,H) ensures no path can reach H via I-C.
    Since all paths from J to F must pass through either E or the H-cluster (H, K, X, G), and we have cut all entry points to both, J is completely disconnected from F.

6.  **Confirm minimality:**
    Let's consider if a cut of size 2 is possible.
    *   If we remove any two edges directly connected to F (e.g., (E,F) and (H,F)), there are still multiple paths remaining (e.g., J-I-H-G-F, J-I-H-X-F, J-I-H-X-K-F).
    *   If we try to cut two "bottleneck" edges from the middle, for example, (B,E) and (I,H):
        *   (B,E) cuts paths through B-E-F.
        *   (I,H) cuts paths through I-H-...-F.
        *   However, paths like J-M-L-E-F (not cut by (B,E)) and J-I-C-H-...-F (not cut by (I,H)) would still exist.
    It is not possible to disconnect J from F with only two edges. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, F), (I, H), (C, H)]

    *   **Necessity of (B, C):** If (B, C) is not removed, a path like N-B-C-H-G would still exist, even if (B, E) and (P, J) are removed.
    *   **Necessity of (P, J):** If (P, J) is not removed, paths like N-O-P-J-I-H-G or N-Y-P-J-I-H-G would still exist, even if (B, E) and (B, C) are removed.

    Since each edge in the set is necessary to break at least one distinct path that would otherwise remain, and their combined removal breaks all paths, this set is a minimal cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

    Since removing any two of the three edges directly connected to M is insufficient to disconnect F from M, and any path to M must use one of these three edges, it implies that a cut of size 2 is not possible.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges that must be removed is 3. The cut set consists of all edges directly connected to node M.

    *   Neighbors of O: C, P, Y

    *   Removing any single node (e.g., B, G, I, C, P, Y, or any other node) will not disconnect H from O, as there are multiple independent routes. For example, if I remove B, paths through G and I still exist. If I remove C, paths through P and Y still exist.
4.  **Initial Assessment for Two Node Cut:**
    *   Consider removing any two of H's direct neighbors (e.g., B and G). Paths through I would remain (e.g., H-I-C-O, H-I-P-O).
    *   Consider removing any two of O's direct neighbors (e.g., C and P). Paths through Y would remain (e.g., H-B-N-Y-O).
    *   This suggests that a cut set of size 2 is unlikely.
5.  **Systematic Search for Vertex-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves). If I find *k* such paths, then at least *k* nodes must be removed.

    Let's identify three vertex-disjoint paths from H to O:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - Y - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N, Y}
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - K - J - D - P - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, D, P}
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}

    Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are disjoint:
    *   {B, N, Y}
    *   {G, K, J, D, P}
    *   {I, C}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are indeed mutually disjoint. This means that any set of nodes that disconnects H from O must contain at least one node from each of these three paths. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

6.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    *   Consider the direct neighbors of H: {B, G, I}. If I remove these three nodes, H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Since H cannot be removed, and B, G, I are its only connections to other non-target nodes, removing them will disconnect H from O.
    *   This set {B, G, I} is a valid cut set of size 3.
    *   Another valid cut set of size 3 would be the direct neighbors of O: {C, P, Y}. Removing these would disconnect O from the rest of the graph.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also found a cut set of size 3 ({B, G, I}) that successfully disconnects H from O. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect F from J.

1.  **Identify the immediate neighbors of F and J:**
    *   Neighbors of F: X, K, G, E
    *   Neighbors of J: M, I, P, D

2.  **Consider paths originating from F:**
    All paths from F to J must pass through one of F's direct neighbors. The relevant neighbors for reaching the rest of the graph are E and G. Nodes X and K are only connected to F and G, so any path through X or K will eventually need to pass through G or E to proceed towards J.

3.  **Evaluate a cut of size 1:**
    *   Removing any single edge (e.g., (F, E), (F, G), (M, J), (I, J), etc.) will not disconnect F from J, as there are always alternative paths. For example, if (F, E) is removed, paths through G still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J). If (M, J) is removed, paths through I and P still exist (e.g., F-E-I-J, F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J). Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Explore cut sets of size 2:**

    *   **Option 1: Cut edges directly connected to F that lead to the rest of the graph.**
        The primary "exit" edges from F's local cluster (F, X, K, G) towards the rest of the graph are (F, E) and (F, G).
        *   If we remove **(F, E)**: All paths from F that go through E are broken (e.g., F-E-M-J, F-E-I-J).
        *   If we remove **(F, G)**: All paths from F that go through G are broken (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J).
        *   By removing both **(F, E)** and **(F, G)**, F becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except for X and K, which are also only connected to F and G, and thus cannot reach J without F or G). This effectively disconnects F from J.
        *   This cut set is `[(F, E), (F, G)]`.

    *   **Option 2: Cut edges that act as "bridges" from F's side to J's side.**
        Consider the set of nodes {F, X, K, G}. The edges connecting this set to the rest of the graph are (F, E) and (G, H).
        *   If we remove **(F, E)**: All paths from F that go through E are broken.
        *   If we remove **(G, H)**: All paths from F that go through G and then H are broken.
        *   Let's verify:
            *   Path F-E-M-J: Broken by (F, E).
            *   Path F-E-I-J: Broken by (F, E).
            *   Path F-G-H-I-J: Broken by (G, H).
            *   Path F-G-H-B-C-I-J: Broken by (G, H).
            *   Path F-G-H-B-N-C-I-J: Broken by (G, H).
            *   Path F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J: Broken by (G, H).
            *   Path F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J: Broken by (G, H).
        *   All paths are broken. This cut set is `[(F, E), (G, H)]`.

    *   **Option 3: Cut edges directly connected to J.**
        J has 4 neighbors: M, I, P, D. To disconnect J by removing its direct edges, we would need to remove all 4 edges: (M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J). This is a cut of size 4, which is not minimal.
        If we try to remove only two edges connected to J (e.g., (M, J) and (I, J)), paths through P and D would still exist (e.g., F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J). Thus, cutting two edges directly connected to J is not sufficient.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified multiple cut sets of size 2 that successfully disconnect F from J. Since we've established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are M and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M from H. I cannot remove M or H.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of M and H to understand the immediate connections:
*   Neighbors of M: L, E, J
*   Neighbors of H: B, G, I

Any path from M to H must start by traversing one of M's neighbors (L, E, or J) and end by traversing one of H's neighbors (B, G, or I).

Let's systematically explore all paths from M to H:

**Paths starting from M:**

1.  **Paths through L:**
    *   M -> L -> E -> B -> H
    *   M -> L -> E -> F -> K -> G -> H
    *   M -> L -> E -> F -> X -> G -> H

2.  **Paths through E (direct connection from M):**
    *   M -> E -> B -> H
    *   M -> E -> F -> K -> G -> H
    *   M -> E -> F -> X -> G -> H

3.  **Paths through J:**
    *   M -> J -> I -> H
    *   M -> J -> D -> P -> I -> H
    *   M -> J -> P -> I -> H
    *   M -> J -> P -> C -> I -> H
    *   M -> J -> P -> C -> B -> H
    *   M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B -> H
    *   M -> J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> H

Now, I will try to find a cut set.

**Attempting a single-node cut:**
*   If I remove L: M can still reach H via E or J. (e.g., M-E-B-H, M-J-I-H)
*   If I remove E: M can still reach H via J. (e.g., M-J-I-H)
*   If I remove J: M can still reach H via L or E. (e.g., M-L-E-B-H, M-E-B-H)
*   If I remove B: Paths through G and I still exist. (e.g., M-E-F-K-G-H, M-J-I-H)
*   If I remove G: Paths through B and I still exist. (e.g., M-E-B-H, M-J-I-H)
*   If I remove I: Paths through B and G still exist. (e.g., M-E-B-H, M-E-F-K-G-H)
Since no single node removal disconnects M and H, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**Attempting a two-node cut:**
Let's consider the nodes directly connected to M: L, E, J.
Notice that L is only connected to M and E. So, any path from M through L must pass through E.
This means all paths from M must pass through either E or J.

*   **If I remove node E:**
    *   All paths starting with M -> L -> E -> ... are broken.
    *   All paths starting with M -> E -> ... are broken.
    *   The only remaining paths from M must start with M -> J -> ...

*   **If I then also remove node J:**
    *   All paths starting with M -> J -> ... are broken.

By removing both E and J, all possible paths originating from M are severed. M's only remaining neighbor is L, but L's only other neighbor is E, which has been removed. Therefore, M becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including H.

This means that removing nodes E and J successfully disconnects M from H. This is a cut set of size 2. Since we've established that a single-node cut is not possible, a cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

    *   If I remove J: Paths like N-O-P still exist.
    *   If I remove F: Paths like N-O-P still exist.
    No single node can disconnect N and P.

4.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum cut size, I can look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between N and P (Menger's Theorem).
    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P (Intermediate node: {O})
    *   **Path 2:** N - B - E - P (Intermediate nodes: {B, E})
    *   **Path 3:** N - C - H - G - F - P (Intermediate nodes: {C, H, G, F})
    These three paths are vertex-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes). This implies that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect N and P.

5.  **Search for a cut set of size 3:** Based on the vertex-disjoint paths, I need to find a set of 3 nodes that severs all connections. A good strategy is to consider nodes that are "bottlenecks" or "entry points" from N's side to P's side.
    Let's consider the direct neighbors of N: {Y, O, C, B}. Any path from N must start through one of these.
    Let's try removing {O, B, C}.

6.  **Verify the cut set {O, B, C}:**
    *   If O is removed: All paths starting N-O-... are broken (e.g., N-O-P, N-Y-O-P, N-O-I-P, etc.).
    *   If B is removed: All paths starting N-B-... are broken (e.g., N-B-E-P, N-B-I-P, etc.). Also, paths N-C-B-... are broken.
    *   If C is removed: All paths starting N-C-... are broken (e.g., N-C-B-E-P, N-C-H-G-F-P, N-C-H-I-P, etc.).

    Let's systematically check all possible paths from N to P after removing {O, B, C}:
    *   **Paths starting N-Y-**: N-Y-O-... (O is removed, so all paths through Y are broken).
    *   **Paths starting N-O-**: O is removed, so all paths through O are broken.
    *   **Paths starting N-C-**: C is removed, so all paths through C are broken.
    *   **Paths starting N-B-**: B is removed, so all paths through B are broken.

    Since all direct neighbors of N (except Y, which leads only to O, which is removed) are removed, no path can originate from N and reach P. Therefore, removing {O, B, C} successfully disconnects N from P.

7.  **Confirm minimality:**
    *   We established that there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths, meaning a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed.
    *   We found a cut set of size 3 ({O, B, C}).
    *   Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes.

    These two paths are node-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes). According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect B and M must be at least 2.

P3: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
P4: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`

According to Menger's theorem, if there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum edge cut is at least `k`. So, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 4.

Now, I will attempt to find a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects E and H. If I can find such a set, then 4 is indeed the minimum.

*   Removing `(F, H)` breaks all paths from E to H that pass through F (e.g., E-F-H). E can still reach F, but F cannot reach H.
*   Removing `(G, H)` breaks all paths from E to H that pass through G (e.g., E-G-H). E can still reach G, but G cannot reach H.

    *   Any path from E through M or L that needs to go through J to reach I or B (and then H) is now blocked.
