*   E's remaining connections: `(E, F)`, `(E, G)`, `(E, L)`, `(E, M)`.

    *   Neighbors of I: H, C, P, J
3.  **Search for vertex-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum vertex cut), I will look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between G and I. By Menger's Theorem, this number is equal to the minimum vertex cut.
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {H}
    *   **Path 2:** G - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (no common intermediate nodes).
    *   **Path 3:** G - E - L - M - D - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {E, L, M, D, P}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common intermediate nodes).

    I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and I. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects G from I. Based on the vertex-disjoint paths, a good strategy is to pick one node from each path.
    Let's try the set **[H, B, E]**:
    *   Removing **H** breaks Path 1 (G - H - I).
    *   Removing **B** breaks Path 2 (G - B - C - I) and all other paths that go through B (e.g., G-B-N-C-I, G-E-B-C-I, etc.).
    *   Removing **E** breaks Path 3 (G - E - L - M - D - P - I) and all other paths that go through E (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I, G-F-E-L-M-J-O-C-I, etc.).

5.  **Verify the cut set [H, B, E]:**
    *   Nodes removed: H, B, E.
    *   Remaining neighbors of G: F, K.
    *   Remaining neighbors of I: C, P, J (H is removed).

    Now, I need to check if any path still exists from G to I.
    *   **Nodes reachable from G (excluding G itself):**
        *   From G, we can go to F or K.
        *   F is connected to X, K. (E is removed)
        *   K is connected to X, F.
        *   So, the component connected to G is {G, F, K, X}.
    *   **Nodes reachable from I (excluding I itself):**
        *   From I, we can go to C, P, J. (H is removed)
        *   C is connected to N, O. (B is removed)
        *   P is connected to J, O, D.
        *   J is connected to M, O, P, D.
        *   N is connected to C, Y. (B is removed)
        *   O is connected to C, P, J, Y.
        *   D is connected to M, P, J.
        *   M is connected to L, J, D. (E is removed)
        *   L is connected to M. (E is removed)
        *   Y is connected to N, O.
        *   So, the component connected to I is {I, C, P, J, N, O, D, M, L, Y}.

    I will now check for any connections between {G, F, K, X} and {I, C, P, J, N, O, D, M, L, Y}.
    *   F, K, X are only connected to each other within G's component. They have no connections to any node in I's component.
    *   Therefore, removing H, B, and E successfully disconnects G from I.

Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3. And I found a cut set of size 3 ([H, B, E]) that successfully disconnects G from I. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and H. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of E and H:
Neighbors of E: L, M, F, B
Neighbors of H: G, B, I

Now, I will systematically explore all paths from E to H. I can categorize these paths based on the first node they take from E, or the last node they take before H.

Let's consider the immediate connections from E and to H:
1.  **Paths through B:**
    *   E - B - H (Direct path)
    *   E - B - C - I - H
    *   E - B - N - C - I - H
    *   E - B - N - O - C - I - H
    *   E - B - N - O - P - I - H
    *   E - B - N - O - P - J - I - H
    *   E - B - N - O - P - J - D - I - H
    All these paths rely on node B. Removing B would sever all of them.

2.  **Paths through F (to G, then to H):**
    *   E - F - G - H
    *   E - F - X - G - H
    *   E - M - F - G - H
    *   E - M - F - X - G - H
    *   E - L - M - F - G - H
    *   E - L - M - F - X - G - H
    All these paths rely on node F to reach G, which is a neighbor of H. Removing F would sever all of them. Alternatively, removing G would also sever all of them, as G is the common node before H in these paths.

3.  **Paths through L or M (not leading to F or B):**
    *   From E, L is connected to M. M is connected to L and F.
    *   If a path goes E -> L -> M, it then must go through F to reach G, or through B to reach H or I.
    *   If a path goes E -> M, it then must go through F to reach G, or through B to reach H or I.
    Therefore, all paths starting with L or M eventually converge to either F or B.

**Analysis for minimum cut:**
*   **Can we disconnect with 1 node?**
    *   If we remove B: All paths in Group 1 are broken. However, paths in Group 2 (E-F-G-H, E-F-X-G-H, etc.) remain. So, B alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove F: All paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 (E-B-H, E-B-C-I-H, etc.) remain. So, F alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove G: All paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 remain. So, G alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove I: Some paths in Group 1 (those going through I) are broken. However, E-B-H and all paths in Group 2 remain. So, I alone is not enough.
    Since no single node can disconnect E and H, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

*   **Can we disconnect with 2 nodes?**
    We have identified two main "bottlenecks" or sets of paths:
    1.  All paths that go through B.
    2.  All paths that go through F (and then G) to reach H.

    To disconnect E and H, we must break all paths in Group 1 AND all paths in Group 2.
    *   To break Group 1, we must remove B.
    *   To break Group 2, we must remove either F or G (since all paths in this group pass through both F and G).

    Therefore, a minimal cut set would consist of B and either F or G.
    Possible cut sets of size 2:
    1.  **[B, F]**:
        *   Removing B breaks all paths E-B-...-H.
        *   Removing F breaks all paths E-...-F-G-H (including those starting with E-M or E-L-M).
        *   After removing B and F, E can only reach L or M. L and M are not connected to G or I (H's remaining neighbors). Thus, E is disconnected from H.
    2.  **[B, G]**:
        *   Removing B breaks all paths E-B-...-H.
        *   Removing G breaks all paths E-...-F-G-H (including those starting with E-M or E-L-M).
        *   After removing B and G, E can only reach L, M, F. F is connected to X, K. None of these are connected to I (H's remaining neighbor). Thus, E is disconnected from H.

Both [B, F] and [B, G] are valid minimal cut sets of size 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, F]

    *   If E is also removed, L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Therefore, removing both M and E will disconnect J from L.

    *   N - O - C - B - H - G

    *   N - B - E - F - K - G

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P.
2.  **Identify direct connections to P:** Node P is directly connected to I, O, J, and D.
3.  **Analyze the role of D:** Node D is only connected to P and J. If J is removed, D becomes a dead-end from P's perspective for reaching N. If J is not removed, any path through D must also pass through J. Therefore, all paths from N to P must ultimately pass through I, O, or J. To disconnect N from P, we must remove nodes such that no path from N can reach any of I, O, or J.
4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If we remove **O**: Paths like N-C-I-P (via I) and N-B-E-M-J-P (via J) still exist.
    *   If we remove **I**: Paths like N-O-P (via O) and N-B-E-M-J-P (via J) still exist.
    *   If we remove **J**: Paths like N-O-P (via O) and N-C-I-P (via I) still exist.
    *   Since removing any single node (I, O, or J) leaves at least two other paths to P intact, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.
5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   If we remove **[O, I]**: Paths to P via O and I are broken. However, paths to P via J still exist, for example, N-B-E-M-J-P.
    *   If we remove **[O, J]**: Paths to P via O and J are broken. However, paths to P via I still exist, for example, N-C-I-P.
    *   If we remove **[I, J]**: Paths to P via I and J are broken. However, paths to P via O still exist, for example, N-O-P.
    *   Since removing any two nodes from {I, O, J} leaves at least one path to P intact, a two-node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.
6.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 3:**
    *   If we remove **[I, O, J]**: All direct connections from the graph to P (except D, which is now isolated from N's side as J is removed) are severed. No path can reach P from N. This set successfully disconnects N from P.
7.  **Conclusion:** Since we have established that a minimum of 3 nodes are required and we have found a set of 3 nodes that achieves disconnection, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [I, O, J]

Image name: PG_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are J and E. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J and E.

2.  **Understand the problem as a min-cut problem:** According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Therefore, I will search for edge-disjoint paths from J to E.

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths from J to E:**
    *   **Path 1:** J → M → L → E
        The edges in this path are: (J, M), (M, L), (L, E).

    *   **Path 2:** J → D → P → O → N → B → E
        The edges in this path are: (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), (B, E).
        Let's verify if these edges are distinct from Path 1's edges:
        (J, D) is distinct from (J, M).
        (D, P) is distinct from (M, L).
        (P, O) is distinct from (L, E).
        All edges are distinct, so Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** J → I → G → F → E
        The edges in this path are: (J, I), (I, G), (G, F), (F, E).
        Let's verify if these edges are distinct from Path 1's and Path 2's edges:
        (J, I) is distinct from (J, M) and (J, D).
        (I, G) is distinct from (M, L) and (D, P).
        (G, F) is distinct from (L, E) and (P, O).
        (F, E) is distinct from (L, E) and (B, E) (the end edges of Path 1 and Path 2 respectively).
        All edges are distinct, so Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

4.  **Determine the lower bound for the cut size:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** A simple way to disconnect node E from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    The edges connected to E are: (E, L), (E, B), and (E, F).
    If these three edges are removed, node E becomes isolated, and no path can exist between J and E.
    The size of this cut set is 3.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 3 edges must be removed) and we identified a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects J and E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, L), (E, B), (E, F)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']]}

    *   Node B is connected to N, C, E, and H.
    *   Node H is connected to B, G, and I.

    Any path from B to H must eventually use one of the edges connected to H. These edges are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H). If we remove all three of these edges, node H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node B. This would be a cut set of 3 edges: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)].

3.  **Verify if this cut set disconnects B and H:**
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks the direct path between B and H.
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that would reach H via node G (e.g., B-N-E-F-G-H, B-E-F-K-G-H).

    With these three edges removed, H has no remaining connections to any other node, ensuring no path exists from B to H.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (i.e., prove minimality):**

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges used: {(B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        Edges used: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 as it uses different edges.

        Edges used: {(B, N), (N, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 as it uses different edges.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, it means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.
    As I have already identified a cut set of 3 edges [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], this set is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and J. I cannot remove E or J.

First, I will identify all possible paths from E to J.
The direct neighbors of E are L, M, F, B.
The direct neighbors of J are M, K, I, P, D. (Node D is a leaf node connected only to J, so it's not relevant for paths from E to J that don't pass through J itself).

Let's list the paths from E to J, grouped by the first node after E:

**Paths starting with E -> L:**
1.  E - L - M - J

**Paths starting with E -> M:**
2.  E - M - J

**Paths starting with E -> F:**
3.  E - F - K - J
4.  E - F - X - K - J
5.  E - F - G - H - I - J
6.  E - F - G - B - C - O - P - J
7.  E - F - G - B - N - Y - O - P - J

**Paths starting with E -> B:**
8.  E - B - C - O - P - J
9.  E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
10. E - B - G - H - I - J

To disconnect E and J, I need to remove nodes such that all these paths are broken.

Let's consider the "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph: M, K, I, P.
If we remove all of these nodes {M, K, I, P}, J would be disconnected from E. This is a cut set of size 4.

Now, let's try to find a smaller cut set.
Consider the immediate neighbors of E: L, M, F, B.
Any path from E to J must pass through one of these nodes.
- If a path starts E -> L, it must then go through M (E-L-M-J).
- If a path starts E -> M, it goes directly to J (E-M-J).
- If a path starts E -> F, it continues through F's connections.
- If a path starts E -> B, it continues through B's connections.

Let's try to identify a cut set of size 1 or 2.
A single node removal is not sufficient, as E has multiple independent connections to J (e.g., E-M-J, E-F-K-J, E-B-C-O-P-J are all distinct initial paths).

Let's try to find a cut set of size 2.
- If we remove M and K: Paths like E-F-G-H-I-J and E-B-C-O-P-J still exist.
- If we remove M and I: Paths like E-F-K-J and E-B-C-O-P-J still exist.
- If we remove M and P: Paths like E-F-K-J and E-F-G-H-I-J still exist.
- If we remove F and B: Paths like E-M-J and E-L-M-J still exist.
- If we remove M and F: Paths like E-B-C-O-P-J still exist.
- If we remove M and B: Paths like E-F-K-J still exist.
It appears that no pair of nodes is sufficient to disconnect E and J.

Let's consider a cut set of size 3.
Consider the nodes {M, F, B}.
1.  Removing M: This breaks all paths that go through M, specifically E-M-J and E-L-M-J.
2.  Removing F: This breaks all paths that start with E-F-..., including E-F-K-J, E-F-X-K-J, E-F-G-H-I-J, E-F-G-B-C-O-P-J, and E-F-G-B-N-Y-O-P-J.
3.  Removing B: This breaks all paths that start with E-B-..., including E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, and E-B-G-H-I-J.

Since all paths from E to J must start with E-L, E-M, E-F, or E-B, and L only connects to M (which is removed), removing M, F, and B effectively severs all connections from E to J.
Therefore, {M, F, B} is a valid cut set of size 3.

To confirm this is the *minimum* size, I can look for node-disjoint paths between E and J. If I can find 3 node-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum cut set size is at least 3.
Let's find three node-disjoint paths (excluding E and J):
1.  Path 1: E - M - J (Intermediate node: {M})
2.  Path 2: E - F - K - J (Intermediate nodes: {F, K})
3.  Path 3: E - B - C - O - P - J (Intermediate nodes: {B, C, O, P})

The sets of intermediate nodes {M}, {F, K}, and {B, C, O, P} are all disjoint.
This confirms that there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between E and J.
Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and J is 3.
Since we found a cut set of size 3 ({M, F, B}), this is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, F, B]

The target nodes are D and F. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect D from F. I will identify all possible paths between D and F and then determine a minimal set of edges whose removal breaks all these paths.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of the target nodes:
Neighbors of D: J, P
Neighbors of F: B, E, K, X, G

Any path from D to F must start from D, pass through one of its neighbors (J or P), traverse a sequence of intermediate nodes, and finally reach one of F's neighbors (B, E, K, X, G) before connecting to F.

To find a minimum cut, I can consider a partition of the graph into two sets: a "source set" (S) containing D, and a "sink set" (T) containing F. The minimum number of edges that cross from S to T is the minimum cut.

Let's define the source set S as all nodes reachable from D without passing through F or any of F's direct neighbors.
Let's define the sink set T as F and all its direct neighbors.
S = {D, J, P, M, O, Y, N, C, I, H, L}
T = {F, B, E, K, X, G}

Now, I identify all edges that connect a node in S to a node in T. These are the "cut edges" for this specific partition.
1.  **(L, E)**: Node L is in S, Node E is in T. (Path example: D-J-M-L-E-F)
2.  **(N, B)**: Node N is in S, Node B is in T. (Path example: D-J-M-O-N-B-F)
3.  **(C, B)**: Node C is in S, Node B is in T. (Path example: D-J-I-C-B-F)
4.  **(H, X)**: Node H is in S, Node X is in T. (Path example: D-J-I-C-H-X-F)
5.  **(H, G)**: Node H is in S, Node G is in T. (Path example: D-J-I-C-H-G-F)

These 5 edges form a cut set. If all these edges are removed, there will be no path from any node in S to any node in T, and thus no path from D to F. Therefore, the maximum flow from D to F is at most 5, and the minimum cut size is at most 5.

Now, I need to verify that this is the *minimum* number of edges. I will do this by showing that removing any one of these 5 edges would leave at least one path between D and F.

*   **If (L, E) is left intact:**
    The path D-J-M-L-E-F would still exist. (Assuming (N,B), (C,B), (H,X), (H,G) are removed).
    Therefore, (L, E) is a necessary edge in the cut set.

*   **If (N, B) is left intact:**
    The path D-J-M-O-N-B-F would still exist. (Assuming (L,E), (C,B), (H,X), (H,G) are removed).
    Therefore, (N, B) is a necessary edge in the cut set.

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - P - O - N
    *   **Path 4:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
    *   **Path 5:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N

    *   Removing (B, N) breaks Path 1.
    *   Removing (C, N) breaks Path 2.
    *   Removing (O, N) breaks Paths 3, 4, and 5, as they all converge to O before reaching N.
    This cut set has 3 edges: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)].

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**

    *   The first edges from H are (H, B), (H, I), and (H, E), which are distinct.
    *   The last edges to N are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N), which are distinct.
    *   The intermediate nodes and edges in these three paths are also distinct (B in Path A; I, C in Path B; E, M, J, P, O in Path C).
    Since these three paths are edge-disjoint, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H and N.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects H and N, and we proved that at least 3 edges are required. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    Let's verify if these three paths are truly edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges connected to F: (F,G), (F,K), (F,E) are distinct.
    *   Edges (G,H), (K,M), (E,B) are distinct.
    *   Edges (H,I), (M,J), (B,C) are distinct.
    *   Edges (I,P), (J,D), (C,O) are distinct.
    *   Edges (P,D), (J,D), (O,D) are distinct edges connected to D.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between F and D, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect F and D is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects F from D. I will try to select one edge from each of the identified edge-disjoint paths such that their removal severs all possible paths.

    Consider the following cut set:
    *   (H,I) - This edge is part of Path 1 (F-G-H-I-P-D).
    *   (M,J) - This edge is part of Path 2 (F-K-M-J-D).
    *   (E,B) - This edge is part of Path 3 (F-E-B-C-O-D).

    Let's verify if removing these three edges [(H,I), (M,J), (E,B)] disconnects F from D:

    *   **Removal of (H,I):** This edge disconnects the "top" branch of the graph (nodes G, H) from the "right" side (nodes I, P, J, D).
        *   Any path starting F-G-H-I... (e.g., F-G-H-I-P-D, F-G-H-I-J-D) is broken.
        *   Any path starting F-X-G-H-I... (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-P-D, F-X-G-H-I-J-D) is broken.

    *   **Removal of (M,J):** This edge disconnects node M from node J.
        *   Any path starting F-K-M-J-D is broken.
        *   Any path starting F-E-L-M-J-D is broken.

        *   Any path starting F-K-M-E-B... (e.g., F-K-M-E-B-C-O-D) is broken.
        *   Any path starting F-E-L-M-E-B... is broken.

    *   All paths through the G-H-I branch are cut by (H,I).
    *   All paths through the K-M-J branch are cut by (M,J).
    *   All paths through the E-B branch (which leads to C, N, O, I, P, J, Y, D) are cut by (E,B).

    Since we found a cut set of size 3, and we previously established that the minimum cut is at least 3, this cut set is indeed minimal.

The final answer is 3 edges.

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are F and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect F and J, without removing F or J themselves.

1.  **Identify immediate neighbors of F:** X, K, G, E.
2.  **Identify immediate neighbors of J:** M, I, P, D.

Let's systematically explore paths from F to J and identify potential cut nodes.

**Consider paths originating from F:**
*   **Paths through E:** F -> E is a direct connection. From E, paths can go to M, L, I, B.
    *   F -> E -> M -> J
    *   F -> E -> L -> M -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> C -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J (and other variations through C, N, O, P to J)

*   **Paths through G:** F -> G is a direct connection. From G, the only way to proceed towards J is through H. (K and X are also connected to G, so any path through K or X would also eventually pass through G and then H).
    *   F -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> E -> M -> J (This path also uses E)
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J (and other variations through C, N, O, P to J)

**Analysis of "bottleneck" nodes:**
Observing the structure, all paths from F must pass through either node E or node G.
If a path goes through G, it must then pass through H to reach any node that can connect to J (e.g., I, B). There are no other connections from G, K, or X to the rest of the graph besides H (and F).

Therefore, every single path from F to J must pass through either node E or node H.

**Testing for a single node cut:**
*   If I remove E: Paths like F -> G -> H -> I -> J would still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
*   If I remove H: Paths like F -> E -> I -> J would still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., I, M, P, C, B, N, O, L, D, K, X, Y), there will always be at least one path remaining between F and J. For example, removing I still leaves F -> E -> M -> J. Removing M still leaves F -> E -> I -> J.

Since a single node removal is not sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**Testing a cut set of size 2: [E, H]**
1.  **Remove E:** All paths that start F -> E -> ... -> J are broken.
2.  **Remove H:** All paths that start F -> G -> H -> ... -> J (or F -> K -> G -> H -> ... -> J, or F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> ... -> J) are broken.

After removing both E and H:
*   F is connected to X, K, G.
*   G is connected to F, K, X.
*   K is connected to F, X, G.
*   X is connected to F, K, G.
None of these nodes (X, K, G) have any remaining connections to the part of the graph containing J.
Therefore, F and J are completely disconnected.

This cut set [E, H] has a size of 2. Since we've established that a single node cut is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of nodes required.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and N.
2.  **Understand the goal:** Find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect H and N.
3.  **Initial check for direct connection:** There is no direct edge between H and N.
4.  **Consider a cut set of size 1:**
    *   Removing G: Paths like H-B-N, H-I-C-N still exist.
    *   Removing B: Paths like H-I-C-N, H-G-K-J-D-P-O-N still exist.
    *   Removing I: Paths like H-B-N, H-G-K-J-D-P-O-N still exist.
    *   Removing C: Paths like H-B-N, H-I-P-O-N, H-G-K-J-D-P-O-N still exist.
    *   Removing Y: Paths like H-B-N, H-I-C-N, H-G-K-J-D-P-O-N still exist.
    *   Removing O: Paths like H-B-N, H-I-C-N, H-G-K-J-I-C-N still exist.
    No single node removal disconnects H and N. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (intuition):** To find the minimum vertex cut, we can look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. If we find *k* vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least *k*.

    Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths between H and N (excluding H and N themselves):
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        *   Intermediate node: {B}
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (B is not I or C).
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - D - P - O - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, D, P, O}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (B is not in this set).
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 2 (I, C are not in this set).

    We have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know the minimum is at least 3, we now look for a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and N.
    Consider the direct neighbors of H: G, B, I. If we remove all of H's direct neighbors, H will be isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus disconnected from N.
    Let's test the cut set: [G, B, I].
    *   If G is removed, H cannot use paths starting with H-G.
    *   If B is removed, H cannot use paths starting with H-B.
    *   If I is removed, H cannot use paths starting with H-I.
    Since G, B, and I are the only direct neighbors of H, removing them ensures that no path can originate from H. Therefore, H is disconnected from N.

7.  **Conclusion:** We have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, proving the minimum cut is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ([G, B, I]) that successfully disconnects H and N. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, K, I, P, D.

2.  **Analyze paths from E to J:**
    Any path from E to J must pass through one of J's neighbors. Let's list J's neighbors and check if they are reachable from E:
    *   **M:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-M, E-L-M, E-F-M, E-G-F-M, E-B-G-F-M).
    *   **K:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-F-X-K, E-G-F-X-K, E-B-G-F-X-K).
    *   **I:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-G-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I).
    *   **P:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-B-C-O-P, E-B-N-Y-O-P).
    *   **D:** Only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D. Therefore, the edge (D, J) is not part of any path from E to J and does not need to be considered for the cut set.

3.  **Identify potential cut edges:**
    Since all paths from E to J must pass through M, K, I, or P, we can consider cutting the edges that connect these nodes to J. These edges are:
    *   (M, J)
    *   (K, J)
    *   (I, J)
    *   (P, J)

    If we remove these four edges, J will be disconnected from M, K, I, and P. Since these are the only nodes reachable from E that connect to J, removing these edges will completely disconnect E from J. This gives us a cut set of size 4: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)].

4.  **Determine if this cut set is minimal:**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum number of edges, we can try to find 4 edge-disjoint paths from E to J. If we can find 4 such paths, then by Menger's theorem, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.

    Let's identify four edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        Edges: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - X - K - J
        Edges: (E, F), (F, X), (X, K), (K, J)
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        Edges: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   The edges (E, M), (E, F), (E, G), (E, B) are all distinct and originate from E.
    *   The edges (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J) are all distinct and terminate at J.
    *   The intermediate edges (F, X), (X, K), (G, H), (H, I), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P) are also distinct and do not overlap with each other or with the start/end edges.

    Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths from E to J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 4. As we have already identified a cut set of 4 edges, this confirms that 4 is indeed the minimum.

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to ensure no path exists between H and E.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.
    *   Node E is connected to: M, F, B, L.

2.  **Explore paths between H and E to find edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut), I can use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from H to E:

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        Edges used: (H, B), (B, E)

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E
        Edges used: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E)
        This path uses edges distinct from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - P - J - M - E
        Edges used: (H, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, E)
        This path uses edges distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.

    Let's verify the edge-disjointness of these three paths:
    *   Path 1: {(H, B), (B, E)}
    *   Path 2: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E)}
    *   Path 3: {(H, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, E)}
    All edges in these three paths are unique and do not overlap. Therefore, these are 3 edge-disjoint paths.

3.  **Determine the lower bound for the minimum cut:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from E.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3 and verify its effectiveness:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all its incident edges. Let's consider removing all edges directly connected to node H:
    *   (H, G)
    *   (H, I)
    *   (H, B)

    Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects H from E:
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths that start from H via G (e.g., H-G-F-E, H-G-X-F-E, H-G-K-F-E).
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start from H via I (e.g., H-I-C-B-E, H-I-P-J-M-E, H-I-C-N-O-Y-L-E).
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks all paths that start from H via B (e.g., H-B-E, H-B-C-I-...).

    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node E. The size of this cut set is 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 3 edges must be removed) and we have found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects H from E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]

1.  **Removing (G, H)**: This directly cuts the path G-H-I. It also cuts any path that starts G-H and then proceeds to I (e.g., G-H-B-C-I).

*   **Size 1:** No single edge can disconnect G from I, as there are clearly multiple independent paths (e.g., G-H-I and G-K-M-J-I).

    *   If we remove (G, H) and (E, B): Paths like G-K-M-J-I would still exist.

Since no combination of two edges can disconnect G from I, and we have found a set of 3 edges that does, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

2.  **Analyze paths through the 'M' branch:**
    *   L - M - F - K - J
    *   L - M - F - G - K - J
    *   L - M - F - X - G - K - J
    All paths from L through M proceed via node F. Therefore, removing the edge (M, F) would disconnect L from all paths that go through M to reach J. Alternatively, removing (K, J) would also cut all these paths, as K is the last common node before J for this branch.

3.  **Analyze paths through the 'E' branch:**
    *   L - E - B - H - G - K - J
    *   L - E - B - N - C - I - J
    *   L - E - B - N - O - P - J
    *   L - E - B - C - I - J
    *   L - E - B - C - O - P - J
    All paths from L through E proceed via node B. Therefore, removing the edge (E, B) would disconnect L from all paths that go through E to reach J.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:** Since all paths from L to J must pass through either the M-branch or the E-branch, we must remove at least one edge from each branch to ensure complete disconnection. This implies a minimum of 2 edges must be removed.

5.  **Construct a minimal cut set:**
    *   To cut all paths in the M-branch, we can remove (M, F). After removing (M, F), node M is only connected to L, effectively isolating L from any further nodes via M.
    *   To cut all paths in the E-branch, we can remove (E, B). After removing (E, B), node E is only connected to L, effectively isolating L from any further nodes via E.

    By removing both (M, F) and (E, B), L becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from J. This set consists of 2 edges.

6.  **Verify:**
    *   If (M, F) is removed, L cannot reach F, K, G, X, and consequently J via the M-branch.
    *   If (E, B) is removed, L cannot reach B, H, G, K, N, C, I, O, P, and consequently J via the E-branch.
    Since all paths from L to J must use either (L, M) or (L, E) as their first edge, and we have effectively blocked all subsequent paths from M and E, L is completely disconnected from J.

This confirms that 2 edges are sufficient and, as established in step 4, necessary.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, F), (E, B)]

        *   N - O - P - I - H
        *   N - O - P - F - G - H
        *   N - O - P - J - M - E - B - H (This path eventually goes through B, which is a common neighbor)
    *   **Paths through C (other than N-C-H):**
        *   N - C - I - H
        *   N - C - I - P - F - G - H
    *   **Paths through B (other than N-B-H):**
        *   N - B - E - P - I - H
        *   N - B - E - P - F - G - H
        *   N - B - E - M - J - P - I - H
        *   N - B - E - M - J - P - F - G - H

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:** To determine a lower bound for the minimum number of edges to remove (by Menger's Theorem), we look for edge-disjoint paths.
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** N - C - H. Edges: `(N, C)`, `(C, H)`.
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** N - B - H. Edges: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`.
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** N - O - P - I - H. Edges: `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`.

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in P1: `(N, C)`, `(C, H)`
    *   Edges in P2: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   Edges in P3: `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`
    All edges listed for P1, P2, and P3 are distinct from each other. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect N and H must be at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since the minimum cut is at least 3, we now look for a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects N and H.
    Consider the edges directly connected to node N: `(N, Y)`, `(N, O)`, `(N, C)`, `(N, B)`.
    If we remove three of these edges, for example, `(N, O)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, B)`, then N will only be connected to Y via the edge `(N, Y)`.
    Node Y is a leaf node, meaning it is only connected to N. Therefore, if N is only connected to Y, N becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including H.

4.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    The proposed cut set is `[(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]`.
    *   Removing `(N, B)` breaks all paths starting with N-B-...
    *   Removing `(N, C)` breaks all paths starting with N-C-...
    *   Removing `(N, O)` breaks all paths starting with N-O-...
    After these three edges are removed, node N is only connected to node Y. Since Y has no other connections, N is effectively disconnected from all other nodes in the graph, including H. This confirms that N and H are completely disconnected.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have established that at least 3 edges must be removed (from Menger's Theorem), and we have found a cut set of exactly 3 edges that achieves the disconnection. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

Image name: Tu_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and E.**
    Let's list the direct neighbors of H: C, I, G.
    Let's list the direct neighbors of E: B, M, P, L.

    We can categorize paths based on the first edge from H or the last edge into E.
    Consider the edges directly connected to E: (E, B), (E, M), (E, P), (E, L).
    -   Node L is only connected to E and M. Any path from H to E via L must first pass through M (e.g., H-...-J-M-L-E). However, a path H-...-J-M-E is shorter and does not require L. If (E, M) is cut, then L is also disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from H. So, (E, L) is not an independent "entry point" for paths from H.
    Therefore, the critical "entry points" into E from the rest of the graph are B, M, and P.

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths.**
    To determine the minimum number of edges required, we can look for edge-disjoint paths between H and E. If we find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - C - B - E
        Edges: (H, C), (C, B), (B, E)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - J - M - E
        Edges: (H, I), (I, J), (J, M), (M, E)
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - P - E
        Edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, P), (P, E)

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from H are (H, C), (H, I), (H, G), which are distinct.
    *   The last edges into E are (B, E), (M, E), (P, E), which are distinct.
    *   Intermediate edges:
        *   Path 1 uses (C, B).
        *   Path 2 uses (I, J) and (J, M).
        *   Path 3 uses (G, F) and (F, P).
    All edges in these three paths are unique to their respective paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size.**
    Since there are at least three edge-disjoint paths between H and E, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and E is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3.**
    We can achieve disconnection by removing the three edges that serve as the "entry points" to E from the main part of the graph:
    *   (B, E)
    *   (M, E)
    *   (P, E)

    Let's verify that removing these three edges disconnects H from E:
    *   Removing (B, E) breaks all paths from H that pass through B to reach E (e.g., H-C-B-E).
    *   Removing (M, E) breaks all paths from H that pass through M to reach E (e.g., H-I-J-M-E).
    *   Removing (P, E) breaks all paths from H that pass through P to reach E (e.g., H-I-P-E, H-G-F-P-E, H-C-N-O-P-E).

    Since all paths from H to E must eventually use one of these three edges to reach E, removing them will completely disconnect H from E. This cut set has a size of 3.

    Alternatively, we could remove the three edges directly connected to H: (H, C), (H, I), (H, G). This would also disconnect H from the rest of the graph, and thus from E, and also has a size of 3.

Since we found a cut set of size 3, and we proved that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (M, E), (P, E)]

Step-by-step reasoning.

First, I will identify all possible paths from E to I. I can categorize these paths by the first white node they encounter after leaving E. The nodes directly connected to E (that lead towards I) are B, F, and M. (Node L is also connected to E, but L does not lead to any path towards I).

Let's list the main branches of paths:

**Branch 1: Paths starting with E -> B**
*   E - B - H - I
*   E - B - C - I
*   E - B - C - O - P - I
*   E - B - C - N - O - P - I
*   E - B - N - O - P - I

**Branch 2: Paths starting with E -> F**
*   E - F - G - H - I
*   E - F - X - G - H - I

**Branch 3: Paths starting with E -> M**
*   E - M - J - I
*   E - M - J - P - I
*   E - M - J - D - P - I

If I remove nodes B, F, and M, then E will no longer have any connections to the rest of the graph (except to L, which does not lead to I). This would effectively disconnect E from I. This is a cut set of size 3: [B, F, M].

Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., of size 1 or 2) exists. To do this, I will try to find the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. If I can find 'k' vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut set size is 'k'.

Let's find vertex-disjoint paths (paths that do not share any intermediate nodes):

1.  **Path 1: E - B - C - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes used: {B, C}

2.  **Path 2: E - M - J - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes used: {M, J}
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {M, J} does not overlap with {B, C}.

3.  **Path 3: E - F - G - H - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes used: {F, G, H}
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (no overlap with {B, C}) and Path 2 (no overlap with {M, J}).

I have successfully identified three vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is at least 3.

Since I found a cut set of size 3 ([B, F, M]) and I have proven that at least 3 nodes must be removed, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

    *   Nodes K, X, and F are only connected to G (and to each other, forming a small sub-graph with G). They do not have any direct or indirect connections to the rest of the graph that could lead to N, other than through G itself.
    *   Nodes E and H are the only two nodes connected to G that also have connections to the broader graph structure which eventually leads to N.

    *   Any path from G to N must necessarily pass through either the edge (G, E) or the edge (G, H). There are no other ways for G to reach the rest of the graph.

4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   **Cut set of size 1:** If I remove only one edge, for example (G, E), then paths through (G, H) still exist (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-H-C-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N).             *   Therefore, removing (G, E) and (G, H) completely isolates G from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we found a valid cut of size 2, this is the minimum number of edges required.

6.  **Alternative cut sets (and why they might be larger or less obvious):**
    *   To disconnect N, one would need to cut all its incoming edges: (N, B), (N, C), and (N, O). This would be a cut of size 3, which is not minimal compared to the size 2 cut found.
    *   Cutting edges in the middle of the graph would require careful analysis of all paths, but the "choke point" directly at G's exit seems to be the most efficient. For example, cutting (H, B) and (E, M) would still leave paths like G-H-C-N or G-H-I-J-P-O-N.

The most efficient way to disconnect G from N is to remove the two edges that connect G to the main body of the graph.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: M, L, F, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: B, G, I.

2.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges:**
    Any path from E to H must eventually pass through one of H's direct neighbors (B, G, or I) via an edge connected to H. Therefore, the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H) are critical for paths reaching H.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I will look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed.

    Let's identify some paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - I - H
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)}

4.  **Check for edge-disjointness:**
    Let's examine the edges used in these three paths:
    *   Path 1 uses (E, B) and (B, H).
    *   Path 2 uses (E, F), (F, G), and (G, H).
    *   Path 3 uses (E, M), (M, J), (J, I), and (I, H).

    Comparing the edge sets, there are no common edges between Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3. They are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

    Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal *does* disconnect E and H.
    Consider the edges directly connected to H: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    *   If I remove (B, H), any path from E that goes through B to reach H is cut.
    *   If I remove (G, H), any path from E that goes through G to reach H is cut.
    *   If I remove (I, H), any path from E that goes through I to reach H is cut.

    By removing these three edges, H becomes isolated from all its neighbors, and thus from E. This set of edges successfully disconnects E and H.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects E and H, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

This means there are four direct edges connecting to J: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).

Consider the strategy of isolating one of the target nodes. If we remove all edges connected to J, J will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This would require removing 4 edges: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)]. This is a valid cut set of size 4.

Now, let's determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., 3 edges) is possible.
To disconnect E from J, every path between them must be broken.

Let's analyze the paths from E to J. All paths to J must pass through one of its direct neighbors: M, D, P, or I.

Consider trying to remove only 3 edges.
**Scenario 1: Removing 3 edges directly connected to J.**
Suppose we remove any three of the four edges connected to J, for example, [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P)].
In this case, the edge (J, I) remains.
We need to check if E can still reach I.
Yes, E can reach I via multiple paths, for example:
*   E - I (direct edge)
*   E - B - H - I
*   E - F - G - H - I
Since E can reach I, and (J, I) is still intact, there is still a path E - ... - I - J. For example, E - I - J.
This means removing any 3 edges directly connected to J is not sufficient.

**Scenario 2: Removing 2 edges directly connected to J and 1 other edge.**
Suppose we remove [(J, M), (J, D)] and one other edge, say (E, I).
The edges (J, P) and (J, I) are still intact.
Can E reach P? Yes, for example, E - B - C - O - P. Since (J, P) is intact, the path E - B - C - O - P - J still exists.
Can E reach I? Even though (E, I) is cut, E can still reach I via E - B - H - I. Since (J, I) is intact, the path E - B - H - I - J still exists.
This means removing 2 edges directly connected to J and 1 other edge is not sufficient.

**Scenario 3: Removing 1 edge directly connected to J and 2 other edges.**
This would be even less effective at isolating J.

**Scenario 4: Removing 3 edges that are NOT directly connected to J.**
Let's try to find a bottleneck further away from J.
Consider the edges connected to E: (E, F), (E, L), (E, M), (E, I), (E, B).
If we remove [(E, M), (E, I), (E, B)]:
*   E - M - J is cut by (E, M).
*   E - I - J is cut by (E, I).
*   Paths through B (e.g., E - B - H - I - J, E - B - C - O - P - J) are cut by (E, B).
However, E is still connected to F and L.
Remaining paths to J:
*   E - L - M - J (still exists, as (L, M) and (M, J) are intact)
*   E - F - G - H - I - J (still exists, as (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J) are intact)
Since at least two paths remain, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

Based on these analyses, it is clear that 3 edges are not enough to completely disconnect E from J. All paths from E to J must pass through one of J's four direct neighbors (M, D, P, I). If we remove only 3 edges, at least one of these connections to J will remain. As long as E can reach that remaining neighbor, a path will exist.

Since 3 edges are insufficient and 4 edges (by removing all edges connected to J) are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

A minimal cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to J.

Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

The target nodes are G (black) and I (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of G and I:**
    *   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X
    *   Neighbors of I: H, P, J, C

2.  **Analyze the overall structure of paths from G to I:**
    All paths originating from G must first pass through one of its neighbors (H, K, F, X).
    All paths terminating at I must pass through one of its neighbors (H, P, J, C).

    Let's trace the possible routes from G:
    *   **Route 1: Through H.**
        *   G - H - I (direct path)
        *   G - H - C - I (indirect path through C)
        Any path from G that goes through H will be cut if we remove the edge (G, H). Alternatively, we could cut the edges (H, I) and (C, I) to sever all connections from H to I.

    *   **Route 2: Through K, F, or X.**
        *   If G goes to K: K is connected to F and X. Both F and X are connected to F. So, G -> K -> F.
        *   If G goes to F: G -> F.
        *   If G goes to X: X is connected to F and K. Both F and K are connected to F. So, G -> X -> F.
        This means all paths from G that do not go through H must eventually pass through node F.
        From F, the only way to reach I is via node P:
        *   F - P - I (direct path from P to I)
        *   F - P - J - I (indirect path from P to I via J)
        Therefore, any path from G that goes through F (which includes paths via K or X) must then pass through P to reach I. This entire "F-P" branch can be cut by removing the edge (F, P). Alternatively, we could cut the edges (P, I) and (J, I) to sever all connections from P to I.

3.  **Identify critical cut points:**
    Based on the analysis above, all paths from G to I must pass through one of two "bottleneck" areas:
    *   Node H (for paths like G-H-I, G-H-C-I)
    *   Node P (for paths like G-F-P-I, G-K-F-P-I, G-X-F-P-I, and their variants through J)

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    To disconnect G from I, we need to cut all paths going through H AND all paths going through P (via F).

    *   To cut all paths through H from G: The most efficient single edge to remove is **(G, H)**. This breaks G-H-I and G-H-C-I.
    *   To cut all paths through P from G (via F, K, X): All these paths converge at F and then go to P. The most efficient single edge to remove is **(F, P)**. This breaks all paths like G-F-P-I, G-K-F-P-I, G-X-F-P-I, and their variants through J.

    By removing these two edges, **[(G, H), (F, P)]**, all identified paths between G and I are broken.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    We have found a cut set of size 2. To confirm it's minimal, we can try to find two edge-disjoint paths between G and I.
    *   Path 1: G - H - I (Edges: (G, H), (H, I))
    *   Path 2: G - F - P - I (Edges: (G, F), (F, P), (P, I))
    These two paths are edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges). According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since we found two edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut must be at least 2. Our cut set of 2 edges is therefore minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, P)]

        *   Removing B breaks Path 3 (E - B - C - I) and any other path starting E-B.
        Since M, F, and B are the only "entry points" from E to the rest of the graph that lead to I, removing them will disconnect E from I. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

    *   **Option 2: Nodes directly connected to I.**
        The neighbors of I are C, P, H. All paths from E to I must end through C, P, or H.
        If I remove nodes {C, P, H}:
        *   Removing C breaks all paths ending in C-I.
        *   Removing P breaks all paths ending in P-I.
        *   Removing H breaks all paths ending in H-I.
        Since C, P, and H are the only "exit points" from the rest of the graph to I, removing them will disconnect I from E. This is also a valid cut set of size 3.

5.  **Conclusion:** Both {M, F, B} and {C, P, H} are valid cut sets of size 3. Since the minimum number of nodes required is 3, either of these sets is a correct answer.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, F, B]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 12
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect M from N.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node M is connected to E, L, and J. (Degree of M is 3)
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, O, and Y. (Degree of N is 4)

2.  **Consider a simple cut set:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges connected to it.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to M: (M, E), (M, L), (M, J). This would isolate M from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from N. This cut set has 3 edges.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to N: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y). This cut set has 4 edges.
    Since we are looking for the *minimum* number of edges, a cut set of size 3 is a strong candidate.

3.  **Prove minimality using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and N. If I can find 3 paths that do not share any common edges, then at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect M and N.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

    *   **Path 2:** M - E - H - B - N
        Edges: {(M, E), (E, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 3:** M - L - E - G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path 1: (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (M,E), (E,H), (H,B), (B,N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (M,L), (L,E), (E,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,C), (C,N)

    By inspecting the edge lists, it's clear that no edge appears in more than one path. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and N, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 3.
    We have already identified a cut set of size 3: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, J)]. This set successfully disconnects M from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

    Since we found a cut set of size 3 and proved that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, J)]
